) above, it fits into the commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{S}\hskip-1.72218pt\mathit{t\hskip-1.07639pti\hskip-1.29167pte\hskip-1.07639ptf\hskip-2.15277pte\hskip-0.86108ptl}^{-}_{\mathcal{O}^{d}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{S}\hskip-1.72218pt\mathit{t\hskip-1.07639pti\hskip-1.29167pte\hskip-1.07639ptf\hskip-2.15277pte\hskip-0.86108ptl}^{-}_{((\mathcal{O}^{d},\mathcal{O}^{d}),h_{d})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}\hskip-1.72218pt\ell\hskip-1.50696pt\textit{a\hskip-1.07639ptg}^{-}_{\mathcal{O}^{d}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}\hskip-1.72218pt\ell\hskip-1.50696pt\textit{a\hskip-1.07639ptg}^{-}_{((\mathcal{O}^{d},\mathcal{O}^{d}),h_{d})}}
(
25
)
(
26
)
(
16
)

where the horizontal maps are isomorphisms and the other downward map is the surjection of
4.6
. Therefore, our map is surjective as well and we are done.
∎

4.B.ii.
Idempotents in Azumaya Algebras with Involution of the Second Kind

Just as in the inner case, the sections of
\mathcal{S}\hskip-1.72218pt\mathit{t\hskip-1.07639pti\hskip-1.29167pte\hskip-1.07639ptf\hskip-2.15277pte\hskip-0.86108ptl}^{-}_{(\mathcal{H},h)}
are related to certain tuples of idempotents in
\mathcal{E}\hskip-1.72218pt\textit{n\hskip-0.86108ptd}_{\mathcal{L}}(f_{*}(\mathcal{H}))
and we may define the appropriate notion for any Azumaya algebra with involution of the second kind. Given an idempotent
e\in f_{*}(\mathcal{B})
, we set
\mathcal{I}_{e}=e\cdot f_{*}(\mathcal{B})\subseteq f_{*}(\mathcal{B})
, which is an
\mathcal{L}
–right ideal which is a direct summand (since
f_{*}(\mathcal{H})=ef_{*}(\mathcal{B})\oplus(1-e)f_{*}(\mathcal{B})
) and therefore a finite locally free
\mathcal{O}
–module.

Definition 4.24
.

Let
(f\colon L\to S,\mathcal{B},\tau)
be an Azumaya algebra with involution of the second kind of constant rank
d
. We say a tuple
(e_{1},\ldots,e_{\ell+1})
of idempotents in
f_{*}(\mathcal{B})
is
\mathcal{L}
–lowered
if for every
T\in\Sch_{S}
and every index
j
,

\mathrm{subrank}_{\mathcal{L}|_{T}}(\mathcal{I}_{e_{j}}|_{T})=0\Rightarrow e_{k}|_{T}=0\text{ for all }k\geq j.

We define the subpresheaf
\mathcal{I}\hskip-1.72218pt\mathit{d\hskip-1.72218pte\hskip-1.50696ptm\hskip-1.50696ptp}^{-}_{(\mathcal{B},\tau)}\subseteq\mathcal{I}\hskip-1.72218pt\mathit{d\hskip-1.72218pte\hskip-1.50696ptm\hskip-1.50696ptp}^{-}_{f_{*}(\mathcal{B})}
to act on
T\in\Sch_{S}
as

T\mapsto\left\{(e_{1},\ldots,e_{\ell+1})\in\mathcal{I}\hskip-1.72218pt\mathit{d\hskip-1.72218pte\hskip-1.50696ptm\hskip-1.50696ptp}^{-}_{f_{*}(\mathcal{B})}(T)\mid\begin{array}[]{l}\text{the tuple is }\mathcal{L}\text{--lowered, and}\\
\tau(e_{i})=e^{\prime}_{\ell+2-i}\\
\text{where }\rho_{\mathcal{I}}(e_{1},\ldots,e_{\ell+1})=(e_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,e_{\ell+1}^{\prime})\end{array}\right\}.

Lemma 4.25
.

The presheaf
\mathcal{I}\hskip-1.72218pt\mathit{d\hskip-1.72218pte\hskip-1.50696ptm\hskip-1.50696ptp}^{-}_{(\mathcal{B},\tau)}
defined above is a sheaf.

Proof.

This follows by an analogous argument as in the proof of
4.20
where one compares the right truncation in
\mathcal{I}\hskip-1.72218pt\mathit{d\hskip-1.72218pte\hskip-1.50696ptm\hskip-1.50696ptp}^{-}_{f_{*}(\mathcal{B})}
to the left truncation in
\mathcal{I}\hskip-1.72218pt\mathit{d\hskip-1.72218pte\hskip-1.50696ptm\hskip-1.50696ptp}^{+}_{f_{*}(\mathcal{B})}
.
∎

Example 4.26
.

Consider the split Azumaya algebra with involution
(f\colon S\sqcup S\to S,(\Mat_{d}(\mathcal{O}),\Mat_{d}(\mathcal{O})),\tau_{d})
of the second kind as in
Equation

6
where the involution
\tau_{d}
is the switch transpose. In this case,
f_{*}((\Mat_{d}(\mathcal{O}),\Mat_{d}(\mathcal{O}))=\Mat_{d}(\mathcal{O})\times\Mat_{d}(\mathcal{O})
and an idempotent in this algebra is of the form

(e,\varepsilon)\in\Mat_{d}(\mathcal{O})\times\Mat_{d}(\mathcal{O})

for idempotents
e,\varepsilon\in\Mat_{d}(\mathcal{O})
. For a section

((e_{1},\varepsilon_{1}),(e_{2},\varepsilon_{2}),\ldots,(e_{\ell+1},\varepsilon_{\ell+1}))\in\mathcal{I}\hskip-1.72218pt\mathit{d\hskip-1.72218pte\hskip-1.50696ptm\hskip-1.50696ptp}^{-}_{((\Mat_{d}(\mathcal{O}),\Mat_{d}(\mathcal{O})),\tau_{d})}

we may localize until all ranks
\rank_{\mathcal{O}}(e_{j}\Mat_{d}(\mathcal{O}))
and
\rank_{\mathcal{O}}(\varepsilon_{j}\Mat_{d}(\mathcal{O}))
are constant and thus

\rho_{\mathcal{I}}((e_{1},\varepsilon_{1}),(e_{2},\varepsilon_{2}),\ldots,(e_{\ell+1},\varepsilon_{\ell+1}))=(e_{1},\varepsilon_{1}),(e_{2},\varepsilon_{2}),\ldots,(e_{\ell+1},\varepsilon_{\ell+1}),

i.e., the section is fixed by
\rho_{\mathcal{I}}
. Now, the symmetry condition says that

(\varepsilon_{i}^{t},e_{i}^{t})=\tau_{d}(e_{i},\varepsilon_{i})=(e_{\ell+2-i},\varepsilon_{\ell+2-i})

or equivalently,

\varepsilon_{i}=(e_{\ell+2-i})^{t}

for all
i
. Thus, our section takes the form

((e_{1},e_{\ell+1}^{t}),(e_{2},e_{\ell}^{t}),\ldots,(e_{\ell},e_{2}^{t}),(e_{\ell+1},e_{1}^{t})).

Hence, when we consider the map of sheaves

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}\hskip-1.72218pt\mathit{d\hskip-1.72218pte\hskip-1.50696ptm\hskip-1.50696ptp}^{-}_{\Mat_{d}(\mathcal{O})}

\displaystyle\to\mathcal{I}\hskip-1.72218pt\mathit{d\hskip-1.72218pte\hskip-1.50696ptm\hskip-1.50696ptp}^{-}_{((\Mat_{d}(\mathcal{O}),(\Mat_{d}(\mathcal{O})),\tau_{d})}

\displaystyle(e_{1},\ldots,e_{\ell+1})

\displaystyle\mapsto((e_{1},(e^{\prime}_{\ell+1})^{t}),(e_{2},(e^{\prime}_{\ell})^{t}),\ldots,(e_{\ell},(e^{\prime}_{2})^{t}),(e_{\ell+1},(e^{\prime}_{1})^{t}))

where
\rho_{\mathcal{I}}(e_{1},\ldots,e_{\ell+1})=(e^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,e^{\prime}_{\ell+1})
, we see that it is injective and locally surjective because sufficiently locally any section is fixed by
\rho_{\mathcal{I}}
. Thus, this map is an isomorphism of sheaves.

\blacksquare

When we consider the case of
(L\to S,\mathcal{B},\tau)=(L\to S,\mathcal{E}\hskip-1.72218pt\textit{n\hskip-0.86108ptd}_{\mathcal{O}|_{L}}(\mathcal{H}),\tau_{h})
for a regular Hermitian form, the sheaf
\mathcal{I}\hskip-1.72218pt\mathit{d\hskip-1.72218pte\hskip-1.50696ptm\hskip-1.50696ptp}^{-}_{(\mathcal{E}\hskip-1.20552pt\textit{n\hskip-0.60275ptd}_{\mathcal{O}|_{L}}(\mathcal{H}),\tau_{h})}
should be isomorphic to
\mathcal{S}\hskip-1.72218pt\mathit{t\hskip-1.07639pti\hskip-1.29167pte\hskip-1.07639ptf\hskip-2.15277pte\hskip-0.86108ptl}^{-}_{(\mathcal{H},h)}
in a fact analogous to
4.9
. To check this, we use the following lemma. Recall that for an
\mathcal{L}
–subspace
\mathcal{V}\subseteq f_{*}(\mathcal{B})
we have the right
\mathcal{L}
–ideal
\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{L},\mathcal{V}}=\Hom_{\mathcal{L}}(f_{*}(\mathcal{B}),\mathcal{V})
.

Lemma 4.27
.

Let
(f\colon L\to S,\mathcal{H},h)
be a regular hermitian form of constant rank
d
and consider the associated Azumaya algebra with adjoint involution of the second kind
(L\to S,\mathcal{E}\hskip-1.72218pt\textit{n\hskip-0.86108ptd}_{\mathcal{O}|_{L}}(\mathcal{H}),\tau_{h})
.

Given an idempotent
e\in f_{*}(\mathcal{E}\hskip-1.72218pt\textit{n\hskip-0.86108ptd}_{\mathcal{O}|_{L}}(\mathcal{H}))
we have an
\mathcal{L}
–submodule
\mathcal{V}_{e}=\Img(e)\subseteq f_{*}(\mathcal{H})
which is a direct summand. Then,

(i)

(\mathcal{V}_{e})^{\perp_{h}}=\mathcal{V}_{\tau_{h}(1-e)}
, and

(ii)

\mathcal{I}_{e}=\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{L},(\mathcal{V}_{e})}
.

Proof.

(i)
: We first check that
\mathcal{V}_{\tau_{h}(1-e)}\subseteq(\mathcal{V}_{e})^{\perp_{h}}
. Indeed, for any
v\in\mathcal{V}_{e}
we have
v=e(v)
and so for any section
x\in f_{*}(\mathcal{H})
we have

h(\tau_{h}(1-e)(x),v)=h(x,(1-e)(e(v)))=h(x,0)=0

and so
\mathcal{V}_{\tau_{h}(1-e)}\subseteq(\mathcal{V}_{e})^{\perp_{h}}
. Conversely, if
x\in(\mathcal{V}_{e})^{\perp_{h}}
, then for all
w\in f_{*}(\mathcal{H})
we have

0=h(x,e(w))=h(\tau_{h}(e)(x),w)

and so
\tau_{h}(e)(x)=0
because
h
is regular. Then,

x=(\tau_{h}(e)+\tau_{h}(1-e))(x)=\tau_{h}(1-e)(x)

which shows that
x\in\mathcal{V}_{\tau_{h}(1-e)}
and so
(\mathcal{V}_{e})^{\perp_{h}}\subseteq\mathcal{V}_{\tau_{h}(1-e)}
. Hence

(\mathcal{V}_{e})^{\perp_{h}}=\mathcal{V}_{\tau_{h}(1-e)}

as claimed.

(ii)
: Since
\mathcal{V}_{e}=\Img(e)
by definition, it is clear that
\mathcal{I}_{e}\subseteq\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{L},\mathcal{V}_{e}}
. Conversely, since
e
is idempotent, if
\varphi\in\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{L},\mathcal{V}_{e}}
then
e\circ\varphi=\varphi
, so
\varphi\in\mathcal{I}_{e}
as well. Therefore,

\mathcal{I}_{e}=\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{L},\mathcal{V}_{e}}.

∎

Lemma 4.28
.

Let
(f\colon L\to S,\mathcal{H},h)
be a regular Hermitian form of constant rank
d
. We have an isomorphism of sheaves

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}\hskip-1.72218pt\mathit{d\hskip-1.72218pte\hskip-1.50696ptm\hskip-1.50696ptp}^{-}_{(\mathcal{E}\hskip-1.20552pt\textit{n\hskip-0.60275ptd}_{\mathcal{O}|_{L}}(\mathcal{H}),\tau_{h})}

\displaystyle\to\mathcal{S}\hskip-1.72218pt\mathit{t\hskip-1.07639pti\hskip-1.29167pte\hskip-1.07639ptf\hskip-2.15277pte\hskip-0.86108ptl}^{-}_{(\mathcal{H},h)}

\displaystyle(e_{1},\ldots,e_{\ell+1})

\displaystyle\mapsto(\mathcal{V}_{e_{1}},\ldots,\mathcal{V}_{\ell+1})

\displaystyle(\pi_{1},\ldots,\pi_{\ell+1})

\displaystyle\;\reflectbox{$\mapsto$}\;(\mathcal{V}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{V}_{\ell+1})

where for a section
(\mathcal{V}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{V}_{\ell+1})\in\mathcal{S}\hskip-1.72218pt\mathit{t\hskip-1.07639pti\hskip-1.29167pte\hskip-1.07639ptf\hskip-2.15277pte\hskip-0.86108ptl}^{-}_{(\mathcal{H},h)}
we set
\pi_{i}
to be the projection onto
\mathcal{V}_{i}
which is zero on all other
\mathcal{V}_{j}
.

Proof.

If this map is well-defined then it is clear it is an isomorphism since it will be the restriction of the isomorphism occuring in
4.9
. Therefore, we must argue that the additional conditions coming from
h
and
\tau_{h}
are respected by this map.

By the commutative diagram in
4.11
, we know that if we denote

\rho_{\mathcal{I}}(e_{1},\ldots,e_{\ell+1})=(e_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,e_{\ell+1}^{\prime})

then we also have that

\rho_{\mathcal{S}}(\mathcal{V}_{e_{1}},\ldots,\mathcal{V}_{\ell+1})=(\mathcal{V}_{e_{1}^{\prime}},\ldots,\mathcal{V}_{e_{\ell+1}^{\prime}}).

Now, starting with a section
(e_{1},\ldots,e_{\ell+1})\in\mathcal{I}\hskip-1.72218pt\mathit{d\hskip-1.72218pte\hskip-1.50696ptm\hskip-1.50696ptp}^{-}_{(\mathcal{E}\hskip-1.20552pt\textit{n\hskip-0.60275ptd}_{\mathcal{O}|_{L}}(\mathcal{H}),\tau_{h})}
we need to show that

\mathcal{V}_{e_{i}}=\left(\bigoplus_{j\neq\ell+2-i}\mathcal{V}_{e^{\prime}_{j}}\right)^{\perp_{h}}.

Since the tuple
(e_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,e_{\ell+1}^{\prime})
consists of pairwise orthogonal idempotents,

\bigoplus_{j\neq\ell+2-i}\mathcal{V}_{e^{\prime}_{j}}=\mathcal{V}_{(\sum_{j\neq\ell+2-i}e^{\prime}_{j})}

and so using
4.27
(i)
we have

\left(\mathcal{V}_{(\sum_{j\neq\ell+2-i}e^{\prime}_{j})}\right)^{\perp_{h}}=\mathcal{V}_{\tau_{h}(1-\sum_{j\neq\ell+2-i}e^{\prime}_{j})}=\mathcal{V}_{\tau_{h}(e^{\prime}_{\ell+2-i})}

where finally
\mathcal{V}_{\tau_{h}(e^{\prime}_{\ell+2-i})}=\mathcal{V}_{e_{i}}
as required due to the fact that
\tau_{h}(e^{\prime}_{\ell+2-i})=e_{i}
because we started with a section in
\mathcal{I}\hskip-1.72218pt\mathit{d\hskip-1.72218pte\hskip-1.50696ptm\hskip-1.50696ptp}^{-}_{(\mathcal{E}\hskip-1.20552pt\textit{n\hskip-0.60275ptd}_{\mathcal{O}|_{L}}(\mathcal{H}),\tau_{h})}
.

Conversely, starting with a section
(\mathcal{V}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{V}_{\ell+1})\in\mathcal{S}\hskip-1.72218pt\mathit{t\hskip-1.07639pti\hskip-1.29167pte\hskip-1.07639ptf\hskip-2.15277pte\hskip-0.86108ptl}^{-}_{(\mathcal{H},h)}
we need to show that

\tau_{h}(\pi^{\prime}_{\ell+2-i})=\pi_{i}

where here as well we have that
(\pi^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,\pi^{\prime}_{\ell+1})
are the projections associated to

\rho_{\mathcal{S}}(\mathcal{V}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{V}_{\ell+1})=(\mathcal{V}^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,\mathcal{V}^{\prime}_{\ell+1}).

By assumption we have that for all
i
,

\mathcal{V}_{i}=\left(\bigoplus_{j\neq\ell+2-i}\mathcal{V}^{\prime}_{j}\right)^{\perp_{h}}=\left(\mathcal{V}_{(\sum_{j\neq\ell+2-i}\pi^{\prime}_{j})}\right)^{\perp_{h}}=\mathcal{V}_{\tau_{h}(\pi^{\prime}_{\ell+2-i})}

once again using
4.27
(i)
. So,
\tau_{h}(\pi^{\prime}_{\ell+2-i})
is another projection onto
\mathcal{V}_{i}
. If it is also zero on all other
\mathcal{V}_{j}
, then it will agree with
\pi_{i}
. However,

\tau_{h}(\pi^{\prime}_{\ell+2-i})(\mathcal{V}_{j})=\tau_{h}(\pi^{\prime}_{\ell+2-i})(\mathcal{V}_{\tau_{h}(\pi^{\prime}_{\ell+2-j})})=0

since
\tau_{h}(\pi^{\prime}_{\ell+2-i})
and
\tau_{h}(\pi^{\prime}_{\ell+2-j})
are orthogonal idempotents. Hence, we conclude that

\pi_{i}=\tau_{h}(\pi^{\prime}_{\ell+2-i})

as required. Thus, the inverse map lands in
\mathcal{I}\hskip-1.72218pt\mathit{d\hskip-1.72218pte\hskip-1.50696ptm\hskip-1.50696ptp}^{-}_{(\mathcal{E}\hskip-1.20552pt\textit{n\hskip-0.60275ptd}_{\mathcal{O}|_{L}}(\mathcal{H}),\tau_{h})}
and so the map is well-defined and we are done.
∎

We may combine the isomorphism in the previous lemma with the surjection of
4.23
and the isomorphism from
3.26
to form the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{I}\hskip-1.72218pt\mathit{d\hskip-1.72218pte\hskip-1.50696ptm\hskip-1.50696ptp}^{-}_{(\mathcal{E}\hskip-1.20552pt\textit{n\hskip-0.60275ptd}_{\mathcal{O}|_{L}}(\mathcal{H}),\tau_{h})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{S}\hskip-1.72218pt\mathit{t\hskip-1.07639pti\hskip-1.29167pte\hskip-1.07639ptf\hskip-2.15277pte\hskip-0.86108ptl}^{-}_{(\mathcal{H},h)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{G}\mathcal{S}\mathcal{B}^{-}_{(\mathcal{E}\hskip-1.20552pt\textit{n\hskip-0.60275ptd}_{\mathcal{O}|_{L}}(\mathcal{H}),\tau_{h})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}\hskip-1.72218pt\ell\hskip-1.50696pt\textit{a\hskip-1.07639ptg}^{-}_{(\mathcal{H},h)}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

Using
4.27
(ii)
, we see that the downward surjection on the left takes the form

(27)

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}\hskip-1.72218pt\mathit{d\hskip-1.72218pte\hskip-1.50696ptm\hskip-1.50696ptp}^{-}_{(\mathcal{E}\hskip-1.20552pt\textit{n\hskip-0.60275ptd}_{\mathcal{O}|_{L}}(\mathcal{H}),\tau_{h})}\twoheadrightarrow\mathcal{G}\mathcal{S}\mathcal{B}^{-}_{(\mathcal{E}\hskip-1.20552pt\textit{n\hskip-0.60275ptd}_{\mathcal{O}|_{L}}(\mathcal{H}),\tau_{h})}

\displaystyle(e_{1},\ldots,e_{\ell+1})\mapsto(0\subseteq\mathcal{I}_{e_{1}}\subseteq\mathcal{I}_{(e_{1}+e_{2})}\subseteq\ldots\subseteq\mathcal{I}_{(e_{1}+\ldots+e_{\ell})}\subseteq f_{*}(\mathcal{H})).

In the general case there is also such a surjection.

Corollary 4.29
.

Let
(f\colon L\to S,\mathcal{B},\tau)
be an Azumaya algebra with involution of the second kind of constant rank
d
. There is a surjective map

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}\hskip-1.72218pt\mathit{d\hskip-1.72218pte\hskip-1.50696ptm\hskip-1.50696ptp}^{-}_{(\mathcal{B},\tau)}

\displaystyle\twoheadrightarrow\mathcal{G}\mathcal{S}\mathcal{B}^{-}_{(\mathcal{B},\tau)}

\displaystyle(e_{1},\ldots,e_{\ell+1})

\displaystyle\mapsto(0\subseteq\mathcal{I}_{e_{1}}\subseteq\ldots\subseteq\mathcal{I}_{(e_{1}+\ldots+e_{\ell})}\subseteq f_{*}(\mathcal{B}))

Proof.

This map locally takes the form of (
27
) and so it is well-defined and surjective.
∎

4.B.iii.
Parabolics and Levis

Consider
\mathbf{G}=\mathbf{U}_{(\mathcal{B},\tau)}
for an Azumaya algebra
(f\colon L\to S,\mathcal{B},\tau)
with involution of the second kind. Here we define an isomorphism between
\mathcal{I}\hskip-1.72218pt\mathit{d\hskip-1.72218pte\hskip-1.50696ptm\hskip-1.50696ptp}^{-}_{(\mathcal{B},\tau)}
and
\mathcal{P}\hskip-1.72218pt\mathcal{L}_{\mathbf{G}}
using cocharacters analogous to what was done in
Section

4.A.ii
. Given a section
\overline{e}=(e_{1},\ldots,e_{\ell+1})\in\mathcal{I}\hskip-1.72218pt\mathit{d\hskip-1.72218pte\hskip-1.50696ptm\hskip-1.50696ptp}^{-}_{(\mathcal{B},\tau)}
we utilize the same cocharacter defined in (
24
), namely

\displaystyle\lambda_{\overline{e}}\colon\mathbb{G}_{m}

\displaystyle\to\mathbf{GL}_{1,f_{*}(\mathcal{B})}

\displaystyle t

\displaystyle\mapsto\sum_{i=1}^{\ell+1}t^{\underline{a}(\overline{e})+1-2i}e_{i}.

where
\underline{a}(\overline{e})\in\underline{\mathbb{Z}}
is the locally constant length of
\overline{e}
. While it is immediate that this cocharacter lands in
\mathbf{GL}_{1,f_{*}(\mathcal{B})}
, we require that it lands in
\mathbf{U}_{(\mathcal{B},\tau)}
. To see this, we utilize
4.12
.

Lemma 4.30
.

Let
(L\to S,\mathcal{B},\tau)
be an Azumaya algebra with involution of the second kind. For a section
\overline{e}\in\mathcal{I}\hskip-1.72218pt\mathit{d\hskip-1.72218pte\hskip-1.50696ptm\hskip-1.50696ptp}^{-}_{(\mathcal{B},\tau)}
, the cocharacter
\lambda_{\overline{e}}
is a cocharacter into
\mathbf{U}_{(\mathcal{B},\tau)}
. That is,

\tau(\lambda_{\overline{e}}(t))=\lambda_{\overline{e}}(t)^{-1}

for all
t\in\mathbb{G}_{m}
.

Proof.

Let
\overline{e}=(e_{1},\ldots,e_{\ell+1})\in\mathcal{I}\hskip-1.72218pt\mathit{d\hskip-1.72218pte\hskip-1.50696ptm\hskip-1.50696ptp}^{-}_{(\mathcal{B},\tau)}
and set
\rho_{\mathcal{I}}(\overline{e})=(e_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,e_{\ell+1}^{\prime})
. By definition, we have that
\tau(e_{i})=e^{\prime}_{\ell+2-i}
for all
i
. Now, we simply compute that

\tau(\lambda_{\overline{e}}(t))=\sum_{i=1}^{\ell+1}t^{\underline{a}(\overline{e})+1-2i}\tau(e_{i})=\sum_{i=1}^{\ell+1}t^{\underline{a}(\overline{e})+1-2i}e^{\prime}_{\ell+2-1}

which by
4.12
is

=\sum_{i=1}^{\ell+1}t^{-\underline{a}(\overline{e})-1+2i}e_{i}=\lambda_{\overline{e}}(t^{-1})=\lambda_{\overline{e}}(t)^{-1},

and so we are done.
∎

Now that we have a cocharacter
\lambda_{\overline{e}}\colon\mathbb{G}_{m}\to\mathbf{U}_{(\mathcal{B},\tau)}
, we can consider its parabolic and Levi pair
(\mathbf{P}(\lambda_{\overline{e}}),\mathbf{L}(\lambda_{\overline{e}}))
.

Theorem 4.31
.

Let
\mathbf{G}=\mathbf{U}_{(\mathcal{B},\tau)}
for an Azumaya algebra with involution of the second kind
(L\to S,\mathcal{B},\tau)
of constant degree
d
. There is an isomorphism of sheaves

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}\hskip-1.72218pt\mathit{d\hskip-1.72218pte\hskip-1.50696ptm\hskip-1.50696ptp}^{-}_{(\mathcal{B},\tau)}

\displaystyle\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}\mathcal{P}\hskip-1.72218pt\mathcal{L}_{\mathbf{G}}

\displaystyle\overline{e}

\displaystyle\mapsto(\mathbf{P}(\lambda_{\overline{e}}),\mathbf{L}(\lambda_{\overline{e}})).

Proof.

We have seen above that this map is well-defined, so it suffices to show that it is an isomorphism locally. Thus, we may assume that

(L\to S,\mathcal{B},\tau)=(S\sqcup S\to S,(\Mat_{d}(\mathcal{O}),\Mat_{d}(\mathcal{O})),\tau_{d}).

In this case

\mathbf{U}_{(\mathcal{B},\tau)}(T)=\{(A,(A^{-1})^{t})\in\Mat_{d}(\mathcal{O}(T))^{\times}\times\Mat_{d}(\mathcal{O}(T))^{\times}\}

and so we have an isomorphism
\mathbf{U}_{(\mathcal{B},\tau)}\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}\mathbf{GL}_{d}
by projecting onto the first factor.

Similarly, given a section
\overline{e}=((e_{1},\varepsilon_{1}),\ldots,(e_{\ell+1},\varepsilon_{\ell+1}))\in\mathcal{I}\hskip-1.72218pt\mathit{d\hskip-1.72218pte\hskip-1.50696ptm\hskip-1.50696ptp}^{-}_{(\mathcal{B},\tau)}
, projection onto the first factor yields a section
(e_{1},\ldots,e_{\ell+1})\in\mathcal{I}\hskip-1.72218pt\mathit{d\hskip-1.72218pte\hskip-1.50696ptm\hskip-1.50696ptp}^{-}_{\Mat_{d}(\mathcal{O})}
under to the isomorphism of
Example

4.26
.

Furthermore, the associated cocharacter of
\overline{e}
can by calculated component wise, i.e.,

\lambda_{\overline{e}}(t)=\left(\sum_{i=1}^{\ell+1}t^{\underline{a}(\overline{e})+1-2i}e_{i},\sum_{i=1}^{\ell+1}t^{\underline{a}(\overline{e})+1-2i}\varepsilon_{i}\right),

where the cocharacter associated to
(e_{1},\ldots,e_{\ell+1})
by (
24
) appears in the first factor (justifying our use of that definition in the inner case). Therefore, this construction is also compatible with the projection onto the first factor. In particular, we have a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{I}\hskip-1.72218pt\mathit{d\hskip-1.72218pte\hskip-1.50696ptm\hskip-1.50696ptp}^{-}_{(\mathcal{B},\tau)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{H}\hskip-1.72218pt\textit{o\hskip-0.86108ptm}_{\textrm{Grp}}(\mathbb{G}_{m},\mathbf{U}_{(\mathcal{B},\tau)})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}\hskip-1.72218pt\mathcal{L}_{\mathbf{G}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{I}\hskip-1.72218pt\mathit{d\hskip-1.72218pte\hskip-1.50696ptm\hskip-1.50696ptp}^{-}_{\Mat_{d}(\mathcal{O})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{H}\hskip-1.72218pt\textit{o\hskip-0.86108ptm}_{\textrm{Grp}}(\mathbb{G}_{m},\mathbf{GL}_{d})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}\hskip-1.72218pt\mathcal{L}_{\mathbf{G}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}

\scriptstyle\sim

where the
\varphi
is the isomorphism of
Example

4.26
and the right side of the diagram sends a cocharacter to its parabolic Levi pair. Then, the composition along the bottom row is the isomorphism of
4.13
and so the composition along the top row, which is our proposed map, is an isomorphism as well.
∎

Corollary 4.32
.

Let
\mathbf{G}=\mathbf{U}_{(\mathcal{B},\tau)}
for an Azumaya algebra with involution of the second kind. The isomorphism of
4.31
fits into the commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{I}\hskip-1.72218pt\mathit{d\hskip-1.72218pte\hskip-1.50696ptm\hskip-1.50696ptp}^{-}_{(\mathcal{B},\tau)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}\hskip-1.72218pt\mathcal{L}_{\mathbf{G}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{G}\mathcal{S}\mathcal{B}^{-}_{(\mathcal{B},\tau)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}\hskip-1.93747pt\textit{a\hskip-0.6458ptr}_{\mathbf{G}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

where the downward maps are those of
4.29
and (
22
) respectively, and the bottom isomorphism is the map of
3.29
.

Proof.

Locally this becomes the diagram of
4.15
.
∎

5.
Severi-Brauer Schemes and Quillen’s Construction

In this final section we extend the classical categorical equivalence between Azumaya algebras and Sever-Brauer schemes to include objects of outer type
A
. This involves defining the notion of an outer type Severi-Brauer scheme in order to correspond to an Azumaya algebra
(\mathcal{B},\tau)
with involution of the second kind. However, we begin by reviewing the correspondence for inner type objects, continuing to use the convention that
d=n+1
.

5.A.
Inner Type

Over our base scheme
S
, a
Severi-Brauer scheme
of constant relative dimension
n
is a scheme
f\colon P\to S
in
\Sch_{S}
such that it is étale locally isomorphic to relative projective space
\mathbb{P}_{S}^{n}\to S
for some
n
. Since
\mathbf{Aut}(\mathbb{P}_{S}^{n})\cong\mathbf{PGL}_{d}
, there is a formal categorical equivalence between Severi-Brauer schemes of constant relative dimension
n
and Azumaya
\mathcal{O}
–algebras of constant degree
d
due to the fact that both of these categories are equivalent to the category of
\mathbf{PGL}_{d}
–torsors. However, there is also a more direct and concrete equivalence between these two categories, namely

(28)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{Azu}_{d}\leftrightarrow

\displaystyle\mathfrak{SB}_{n}

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}\mapsto

\displaystyle\SB(\mathcal{A})

\displaystyle f_{*}\big(\mathcal{E}\hskip-1.72218pt\textit{n\hskip-0.86108ptd}_{\mathcal{O}|_{P}}(\mathcal{F}(P))\big)\reflectbox{$\mapsto$}

\displaystyle P.

Some explanation of notation is required. The Severi-Brauer scheme of
\mathcal{A}
, denoted
\SB(\mathcal{A})
, is the scheme of finite locally free right ideals in
\mathcal{A}
of constant rank
d
which are locally direct summands. Framing this in our context, since
\mathcal{A}
is of inner type
A_{n}
, we have the commutative diagram of
2.26

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{G}\mathcal{S}\mathcal{B}^{-}_{\mathcal{A}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}\hskip-1.93747pt\textit{a\hskip-0.6458ptr}_{\mathbf{GL}_{1,\mathcal{A}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t_{\mathcal{G}\mathcal{S}\mathcal{B}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\hskip 6.02777pt}^{c}}

where
\mathcal{P}_{n}=\mathcal{O}\hskip-1.29167pt\textit{f}(\mathcal{D}\hskip-1.72218pt\textit{y\hskip-1.29167ptn}(\mathbf{GL}_{1,\mathcal{A}}))
. Since
\mathcal{P}\hskip-1.93747pt\textit{a\hskip-0.6458ptr}_{\mathbf{GL}_{1,\mathcal{A}}}
is representable as mentioned in
Section

1.E
so is
\mathcal{G}\mathcal{S}\mathcal{B}^{-}_{\mathcal{A}}
since the top map in the diagram is the isomorphism of
2.22
. Say
\mathcal{G}\mathcal{S}\mathcal{B}^{-}_{\mathcal{A}}
is represented by
X\in\Sch_{S}
. The sheaf
\mathcal{P}_{n}
is represented by
(\mathscr{P}_{n})_{S}
which is
2^{n}
disjoint copies of
S
, indexed by subsets of
\{1,\ldots,n\}
written as increasing tuples. Thus we have a map of schemes

X\to\bigsqcup_{r\in\mathscr{P}_{n}}S_{r}

corresponding to
t_{\mathcal{G}\mathcal{S}\mathcal{B}}
where all
S_{r}=S
. The type morphism sends a flag of ideals to its tuple of ranks divided by
d
, and so if we consider the fiber of this map over
S_{(1)}
, we obtain a scheme

f\colon X_{(1)}\to S

which represents finite locally free right ideals in
\mathcal{A}
of constant rank
d
which are locally direct summands. We define
\SB(\mathcal{A})=X_{(1)}
. It is well known that if we start with
\mathcal{A}=\Mat_{d}(\mathcal{O})
, then
X_{(1)}\cong\mathbb{P}_{S}^{n}
and so the schemes produced through this process for general Azumaya
\mathcal{O}
–algebras are indeed Severi-Brauer schemes.

In the other direction, we have Quillen’s construction, which we review from
[
Kol
, Theorem 81]
. Given a Severi-Brauer scheme
f\colon P\to S
, there is a unique non-split extension of
\mathcal{O}|_{P}
–modules

0\to\mathcal{O}|_{P}\to\mathcal{F}(P)\to\mathcal{T}_{P/S}\to 0

where
\mathcal{T}_{P/S}
is the tangent bundle of
P
over
S
. Kollár notes that the bundle
\mathcal{F}(P)^{*}
, i.e., the dual, appears in
[
Qui
]
, leading to the name of the construction. When
P=\mathbb{P}_{S}^{n}
, this unique non-split extension takes the form

0\to\mathcal{O}|_{\mathbb{P}_{S}^{n}}\to\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}_{S}^{n}}(1)^{d}\to\mathcal{T}_{\mathbb{P}_{S}^{n}/S}\to 0

which is the Euler sequence for relative projective space. Since
\mathcal{F}(P)
locally becomes isomorphic to
\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}_{S}^{n}}(1)^{d}
, it is constant rank
d
and thus the endomorphism algebra
\mathcal{E}\hskip-1.72218pt\textit{n\hskip-0.86108ptd}_{\mathcal{O}|_{P}}(\mathcal{F}(P))
is an Azumaya algebra over
P
of constant degree
d
. The pushforward retains this property, and so we set

\mathcal{A}_{P}=f_{*}(\mathcal{E}\hskip-1.72218pt\textit{n\hskip-0.86108ptd}_{\mathcal{O}|_{P}}(\mathcal{F}(P)))

which is called the Azumaya
\mathcal{O}
–algebra associated to
P
. In the case of
\mathbb{P}_{S}^{n}
it is clear that
\mathcal{E}\hskip-1.72218pt\textit{n\hskip-0.86108ptd}_{\mathcal{O}|_{\mathbb{P}_{S}^{n}}}(\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}_{S}^{n}}(1)^{d})\cong\Mat_{d}(\mathcal{O}|_{\mathbb{P}_{S}^{n}})
and so
\mathcal{A}_{\mathbb{P}_{S}^{n}}=\Mat_{d}(\mathcal{O})
, showing that for general Severi-Brauer schemes
P
the algebra
\mathcal{A}_{P}
is indeed an Azumaya
\mathcal{O}
–algebra.

Let
P
be a Severi-Brauer scheme. Using the equivalence between Severi-Brauer schemes and Azumaya algebras, say
P=\SB(\mathcal{A})
, we define the opposite Severi-Brauer scheme to be
P^{\mathrm{op}}=\SB(\mathcal{A}^{\mathrm{op}})
. In
[
Kol
]
(for Severi-Brauer varities over a field), this is called the dual and denoted
P^{\vee}
. Given a right ideal
\mathcal{J}\subset\mathcal{A}^{\mathrm{op}}
of constant rank
d
which is locally a direct summand, its left annihilator
{{}^{0}}\mathcal{J}
is a left ideal of
\mathcal{A}^{\mathrm{op}}
of constant rank
d^{2}-d=d(d-1)=dn
which is also locally a direct summand. Then, since left ideals in
\mathcal{A}^{\mathrm{op}}
are also right ideals in
\mathcal{A}
itself, this corresponds to a section of
\mathcal{G}\mathcal{S}\mathcal{B}^{-}_{\mathcal{A}}
with type
(n)
. Therefore, we see that

\SB(\mathcal{A}^{\mathrm{op}})\cong X_{(n)}

is also a fiber of the map
X\to\sqcup_{r\in\mathscr{P}_{n}}S_{r}
corresponding to
t_{\mathcal{G}\mathcal{S}\mathcal{B}}
.

5.B.
A Variation on Quillen’s Construction

Here we will work with relative projective space and various vector bundles on relative projective space and we will twist both the scheme and vector bundles simultaneously. In order to keep track of this simultaneous twisting and not get lost in isomorphisms, we will work with these objects’ incarnations as stacks over
\Sch_{S}
. The punchline of this section is that for a Severi-Brauer scheme
f\colon P\to S
, the pushforward
f_{*}(\mathcal{F}(P))
can be given an algebra structure without first taking the endomorphism algebra, and
f_{*}(F(\SB(A)))\cong A
.

5.B.i.
The Relative Projective Stack

For any
S
–scheme
f\colon X\to S
, the functor
\Sch_{X}\to\Sch_{S}
sending an
X
–scheme
g\colon U\to X
to the
S
–scheme
f\circ g\colon U\to S
and which acts obviously on morphisms, gives
\Sch_{X}
the structure of a stack over
\Sch_{S}
. This is the same stack one obtains when constructing a stack from the sheaf
h_{X}\colon\Sch_{S}\to\Sets
represented by
X
. In the case of relative projective space
\mathbb{P}_{S}^{n}\to S
, we may use the characterization of morphisms
U\to\mathbb{P}_{S}^{n}
from
[
St
, Tag 01NE]
and consider the stack

\mathbb{P}^{n}\to\Sch_{S}

with

(i)

objects
(U,\mathcal{L},(s_{0},\ldots,s_{n}))
where
U\in\Sch_{S}
,
\mathcal{L}\colon\Sch_{U}\to\Ab
is a line bundle, and
s_{0},\ldots,s_{n}\in\mathcal{L}(U)
are sections which globally generate
\mathcal{L}
,

(ii)

morphisms
(g,\psi)\colon(U^{\prime},\mathcal{L}^{\prime},(s_{0}^{\prime},\ldots,s_{n}^{\prime}))\to(U,\mathcal{L},(s_{0},\ldots,s_{n}))
where
g\colon U^{\prime}\to U
is a morphism in
\Sch_{S}
and
\psi\colon\mathcal{L}|_{U^{\prime}}\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}\mathcal{L}^{\prime}
is an isomorphism of
\mathcal{O}|_{U^{\prime}}
–line bundles such that
\psi(s_{i}|_{U^{\prime}})=s_{i}^{\prime}
, and

(iii)

structure morphism sending
(U,\mathcal{L},(s_{0},\ldots,s_{n}))\mapsto U
and
(g,\psi)\mapsto g
.

This is not exactly the stack
\Sch_{\mathbb{P}_{S}^{n}}\to\Sch_{S}
, since in any stack of the form
\Sch_{X}\to\Sch_{S}
the fiber over
U
does not contain any non-identity isomorphisms, while in
\mathbb{P}^{n}(U)
we have isomorphisms of the form

(U,\mathcal{L},(s_{0},\ldots,s_{n}))\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}(U,\mathcal{L},(as_{0},\ldots,as_{n}))

for
a\in\mathbb{G}_{m}(U)
. Nevertheless, the automorphism group of any object in
\mathbb{P}^{n}(U)
is trivial and so there is an equivalence of stacks
\Sch_{\mathbb{P}_{S}^{n}}\cong\mathbb{P}^{n}
, so we choose to work with
\mathbb{P}^{n}\to\Sch_{S}
in order to avoid working with equivalence classes of tuples up to scaling. Relative projective space
\mathbb{P}_{S}^{n}
has the line bundle
\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}_{S}^{n}}(1)
with globally generating sections
x_{0},\ldots,x_{n}
. A morphism of schemes
U\to\mathbb{P}_{S}^{n}
in
\Sch_{S}
corresponds to the object
(U,\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}_{S}^{n}}(1)|_{U},(x_{0}|_{U},\ldots,x_{n}|_{U}))\in\mathbb{P}^{n}
where the restriction to
U
is along the given morphism.

The automorphism group of relative projective space is
\mathbf{Aut}_{S}(\mathbb{P}_{S}^{n})=\mathbf{PGL}_{d}
and it acts on the stack
\mathbb{P}^{n}\to\Sch_{S}
in the following way. Consider
\varphi\in\mathbf{PGL}_{d}(S)
. The exact sequence of groups
1\to\mathbb{G}_{m}\to\mathbf{GL}_{d}\to\mathbf{PGL}_{d}\to 1
gives us a long exact sequence in cohomology with first connecting morphism

\mathbf{PGL}_{d}(S)\xrightarrow{\delta}\Pic(S).

Let
\delta(\varphi)=\mathcal{I}
. This means that there exists an isomorphism
A\colon\mathcal{O}^{d}\to\mathcal{I}^{d}
of
\mathcal{O}
–modules such that the induced isomorphism on the endomorphism algebras

\displaystyle\Mat_{d}(\mathcal{O})=\mathcal{E}\hskip-1.72218pt\textit{n\hskip-0.86108ptd}_{\mathcal{O}}(\mathcal{O}^{d})

\displaystyle\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}\mathcal{E}\hskip-1.72218pt\textit{n\hskip-0.86108ptd}_{\mathcal{O}}(\mathcal{I}^{d})=\Mat_{d}(\mathcal{O})

\displaystyle T

\displaystyle\mapsto ATA^{-1}

is the isomorphism
\varphi
, viewing
\mathbf{PGL}_{d}=\mathbf{Aut}(\Mat_{d}(\mathcal{O}))
. We may also view this more literally as matrix multiplication. The map
A
can be written as a matrix
A\in\Mat_{d}(\mathcal{I})
with entries in
\mathcal{I}
, and then its inverse
A^{-1}\in\Mat_{d}(\mathcal{I}^{*})
has entries in the dual line bundle. The product
ATA^{-1}
can then be calculated as usual where the entries of
A^{-1}
act by evaluation when encountering a section of
\mathcal{I}
.

For any object
(U,\mathcal{L},(s_{0},\ldots,s_{n}))\in\mathbb{P}^{n}
, by restricting and tensoring we get an isomorphism

A|_{U}\otimes 1\colon\mathcal{L}^{d}=\mathcal{O}|_{U}^{d}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}|_{U}}\mathcal{L}\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}\mathcal{I}_{U}^{d}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}|_{U}}\mathcal{L}=(\mathcal{I}|_{U}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}|_{U}}\mathcal{L})^{d}

which sends the section
(s_{0},\ldots,s_{n})
to some section
(s_{0}^{\prime},\ldots,s_{n}^{\prime})\in(\mathcal{I}|_{U}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}|_{U}}\mathcal{L})^{d}
. The section
\varphi\in\mathbf{PGL}_{d}
then acts on
\mathbb{P}^{n}
as a
\Sch_{S}
–stack isomorphism by

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}^{n}

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\varphi}\mathbb{P}^{n}

\displaystyle(U,\mathcal{L},(s_{0},\ldots,s_{n}))

\displaystyle\mapsto(U,\mathcal{I}|_{U}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}|_{U}}\mathcal{L},(s_{0}^{\prime},\ldots,s_{n}^{\prime}))

\displaystyle(g,\psi)

\displaystyle\mapsto(g,1\otimes\psi).

5.B.ii.
Some Vector Bundles on
\mathbb{P}^{n}

Any sheaf
\mathcal{F}\colon\Sch_{X}\to\Sets
can be viewed as a stack over
\Sch_{X}
by placing each section of
\mathcal{F}(U)
as an object over
U\in\Sch_{S}
and adding morphisms to encode restrictions. We do this for the sheaves
\mathcal{O}|_{\mathbb{P}_{S}^{n}}
,
\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}_{S}^{n}}(1)
, and the tangent bundle
\mathcal{T}_{\mathbb{P}_{S}^{n}/S}
on
\Sch_{\mathbb{P}_{S}^{n}}
and we write them as stacks over
\mathbb{P}^{n}
. We abuse notation and denote the resulting stacks the same way.

For the structure sheaf, the global sections
\mathcal{O}(U)
are intrinsic to
U
and do not depend on the chosen morphism to
\mathbb{P}_{S}^{n}
, and so as a stack it becomes

\mathcal{O}|_{\mathbb{P}^{n}}\to\mathbb{P}^{n}

with

(i)

objects
(U,\mathcal{L},(s_{0},\ldots,s_{n}),a\in\mathcal{O}(U))
where
(U,\mathcal{L},(s_{0},\ldots,s_{n}))\in\mathbb{P}^{n}
,

(ii)

morphisms

(g,\psi)\colon(U^{\prime},\mathcal{L}^{\prime},(s_{0}^{\prime},\ldots,s_{n}^{\prime}),a^{\prime}\in\mathcal{O}(U^{\prime}))\to(U,\mathcal{L},(s_{0},\ldots,s_{n}),a\in\mathcal{O}(U))

where
(g,\psi)
is a morphism between the underlying objects in
\mathbb{P}^{n}
such that
a|_{U^{\prime}}=a^{\prime}
, and

(iii)

with the obvious structure morphism to
\mathbb{P}^{n}
.

For the line bundle
\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}_{S}^{n}}(1)
, the interpretation of an object
(U,\mathcal{L},(s_{0},\ldots,s_{n}))\in\mathbb{P}^{n}
is that
\mathcal{L}
is the restriction of
\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}_{S}^{n}}(1)
to
U
. Therefore, the appropriate stack of sections is

\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{n}}(1)\to\mathbb{P}^{n}

with

(i)

objects
(U,\mathcal{L},(s_{0},\ldots,s_{n}),\ell\in\mathcal{L}(U))
where
(U,\mathcal{L},(s_{0},\ldots,s_{n}))\in\mathbb{P}^{n}
,

(ii)

morphisms

(g,\psi)\colon(U^{\prime},\mathcal{L}^{\prime},(s_{0}^{\prime},\ldots,s_{n}^{\prime}),\ell^{\prime}\in\mathcal{L}^{\prime}(U^{\prime}))\to(U,\mathcal{L},(s_{0},\ldots,s_{n}),\ell\in\mathcal{L}(U))

where
(g,\psi)
is a morphism between the underlying objects in
\mathbb{P}^{n}
such that
\psi(\ell|_{U^{\prime}})=\ell^{\prime}
, and

(iii)

with the obvious structure morphism to
\mathbb{P}^{n}
.

The vector bundle
\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{n}}(1)^{d}
then appears similarly, where we instead have objects of the form
(U,\mathcal{L},(s_{0},\ldots,s_{n}),v\in\mathcal{L}^{d}(U))
.

Finally, for any morphism of schemes
X\to S
, the tangent bundle
\mathcal{T}_{X/S}\colon\Sch_{X}\to\Sets
can be defined by
\mathcal{T}_{X/S}(Y)=\Ker(h_{X}(Y[\varepsilon])\to h_{X}(Y))
where
Y[\varepsilon]
is the scheme of dual numbers over
Y
and the morphism
Y\to Y[\varepsilon]
is given by sending
\varepsilon\mapsto 0
within the structure sheaves. In the context of projective space, this means that the tangent stacks is

\mathcal{T}_{\mathbb{P}^{n}/S}\to\mathbb{P}^{n}

with

(i)

objects
(U,\mathcal{L},(s_{0},\ldots,s_{n}),\mathcal{J},(j_{0},\ldots,j_{n}))
where
(U,\mathcal{L},(s_{0},\ldots,s_{n}))\in\mathbb{P}^{n}
and
\mathcal{J}
is a line bundle of
U[\varepsilon]
globally generated by
j_{0},\ldots,j_{n}
and such that
\mathcal{J}|_{U}=\mathcal{L}
and
j_{i}|_{U}=s_{i}
,

(ii)

morphisms

(g,\psi,\rho)\colon(U^{\prime},\mathcal{L}^{\prime},(s_{0}^{\prime},\ldots,s_{n}^{\prime}),\mathcal{J}^{\prime},(j_{0}^{\prime},\ldots,j_{n}^{\prime}))\to(U,\mathcal{L},(s_{0},\ldots,s_{n}),\mathcal{J},(j_{0},\ldots,j_{n}))

where
(g,\psi)
is a morphism between the underlying objects in
\mathbb{P}^{n}
and
\rho\colon\mathcal{J}|_{U^{\prime}[\varepsilon]}\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}\mathcal{J}
is an isomorphism of
U^{\prime}[\varepsilon]
–line bundles such that we have
\rho(j_{k}|_{U^{\prime}[\varepsilon]})=j_{k}^{\prime}
, which also implies that
\rho|_{U}=\psi
, and

(iii)

with the obvious structure morphism to
\mathbb{P}^{n}
.

Similar to
\mathbb{P}^{n}
itself, while this stack does not have any non-trivial automorphisms, it does have some non-trivial isomorphisms in the fiber over an object in
\mathbb{P}^{n}
. Namely, for any section
b\in\mathcal{O}(U)
we may scale by
1+b\varepsilon\in\mathbb{G}_{m}(U[\varepsilon])
and obtain an isomorphism

(U,\mathcal{L},(s_{0},\ldots,s_{n}),\mathcal{J},(j_{0},\ldots,j_{n}))\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}(U,\mathcal{L},(s_{0},\ldots,s_{n}),\mathcal{J},(j_{0}+b\varepsilon j_{0},\ldots,j_{n}+b\varepsilon j_{n}))

in the fiber
\mathcal{T}_{\mathbb{P}^{n}/S}((U,\mathcal{L},(s_{0},\ldots,s_{n}))
. This appears as something more familiar for certain objects in this fiber, namely those where
\mathcal{J}=\mathcal{L}\oplus\varepsilon\mathcal{L}
. All such objects take the form

(U,\mathcal{L},(s_{0},\ldots,s_{n}),\mathcal{L}+\varepsilon\mathcal{L},(s_{0}+\varepsilon t_{0},\ldots,s_{n}+\varepsilon t_{n}))

and the above object is isomorphic to all objects of the form

(U,\mathcal{L},(s_{0},\ldots,s_{n}),\mathcal{L}+\varepsilon\mathcal{L},(s_{0}+\varepsilon(t_{0}+bs_{0}),\ldots,s_{n}+\varepsilon(t_{n}+bs_{n})))

for all
b\in\mathcal{O}(U)
. That is to say, the isomorphism class containing these objects can be interpreted as the section
\overline{(t_{0},\ldots,t_{n})}\in\left(\mathcal{L}^{d}/\langle(s_{0},\ldots,s_{n})\rangle\right)(U)
, where one may like to intuitively think of
\mathcal{L}^{d}/\langle(s_{0},\ldots,s_{n})\rangle
as the vector bundle perpendicular to the vector
(s_{0},\ldots,s_{n})
.

The Euler sequence for relative projective space can then be written in terms of
\mathbb{P}^{n}
–stacks morphisms between objects. The map
\mathcal{O}|_{\mathbb{P}_{S}^{n}}\to\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}_{S}^{n}}(1)^{d}
takes the form

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}|_{\mathbb{P}^{n}}

\displaystyle\to\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{n}}(1)^{d}

\displaystyle(U,\mathcal{L},(s_{0},\ldots,s_{n}),a\in\mathcal{O}(U))

\displaystyle\mapsto(U,\mathcal{L},(s_{0},\ldots,s_{n}),(as_{0},\ldots,as_{n})\in\mathcal{L}^{d}(U))

and it behaves as one would expected on morphisms. The map
\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}_{S}^{n}}(1)^{d}\to\mathcal{T}_{\mathbb{P}_{S}^{n}/S}
appears in terms of stacks as

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{n}}(1)^{d}

\displaystyle\to\mathcal{T}_{\mathbb{P}^{n}/S}

\displaystyle(U,\mathcal{L},(s_{0},\ldots,s_{n}),v\in\mathcal{L}^{d}(U))

\displaystyle\to(U,\mathcal{L},(s_{0},\ldots,s_{n}),\mathcal{L}\oplus\varepsilon\mathcal{L},(s_{0}+\varepsilon v_{0},\ldots,s_{n}+\varepsilon v_{n}))

where
v_{i}
are the coordinates of
v
. In the intuitive interpretation given above, this simply sends
v\in\mathcal{L}^{d}(U)
to
\overline{v}\in\left(\mathcal{L}^{d}/\langle(s_{0},\ldots,s_{n})\rangle\right)(U)
.

5.B.iii.
Twisting by
\mathbf{PGL}_{d}
Actions

For each of
\mathcal{O}|_{\mathbb{P}^{n}}
,
\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{n}}(1)^{d}
, and
\mathcal{T}_{\mathbb{P}^{n}/S}
we define an action of
\mathbf{PGL}_{d}
by
\Sch_{S}
–stack morphisms which act compatibly with the
\mathbf{PGL}_{d}
action on
\mathbb{P}^{n}
itself.

Let
\varphi\in\mathbf{PGL}_{d}(S)
with associated
\mathcal{O}
–module isomorphism
A\colon\mathcal{O}^{d}\to\mathcal{I}^{d}
for a line bundle
\mathcal{I}
on
S
. Recall that it acts on
\mathbb{P}^{n}
by

(U,\mathcal{L},(s_{0},\ldots,s_{n}))\mapsto(U,\mathcal{I}|_{U}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}|_{U}}\mathcal{L},(s_{0}^{\prime},\ldots,s_{n}^{\prime}))

where the
s_{i}^{\prime}
are the coordinates of
(A\otimes 1)(s_{0},\ldots,s_{n})\in(\mathcal{I}|_{U}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}|_{U}}\mathcal{L})^{d}(U)
. The section
\varphi
acts naturally on the structure sheaf, simply by

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}|_{\mathbb{P}^{n}}

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\varphi}\mathcal{O}|_{\mathbb{P}^{n}}

\displaystyle(U,\mathcal{L},(s_{0},\ldots,s_{n}),a\in\mathcal{O}(U))

\displaystyle\mapsto(U,\mathcal{I}|_{U}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}|_{U}}\mathcal{L},(s_{0}^{\prime},\ldots,s_{n}^{\prime}),a\in\mathcal{O}(U)).

We define the action of
\varphi
on
\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{n}}(1)^{d}
by

(29)

\displaystyle\varphi(U,\mathcal{L},(s_{0},\ldots,s_{n}),v\in\mathcal{L}^{d}(U))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(U,\mathcal{I}|_{U}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}|_{U}}\mathcal{L},(s_{0}^{\prime},\ldots,s_{n}^{\prime}),(A\otimes 1)(v)\in(\mathcal{I}|_{U}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}|_{U}}\mathcal{L})^{d}(U)).

Finally, the definition of
\mathcal{T}_{\mathbb{P}^{n}/S}
as consisting of morphisms in
\mathbb{P}^{n}
belonging to certain kernels means that the morphism
\varphi\colon\mathbb{P}^{n}\to\mathbb{P}^{n}
naturally induces a compatible morphism on
\mathcal{T}_{\mathbb{P}^{n}/S}
. Namely, this is

\displaystyle\varphi(U,\mathcal{L},(s_{0},\ldots,s_{n}),\mathcal{J},(j_{0},\ldots,j_{n}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(U,\mathcal{I}|_{U}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}|_{U}}\mathcal{L},(s_{0}^{\prime},\ldots,s_{n}^{\prime}),\mathcal{I}|_{U[\varepsilon]}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}|_{U[\varepsilon]}}\mathcal{J},(j_{0}^{\prime},\ldots,j_{n}^{\prime}))

where
j_{k}^{\prime}
are the coordinates of
(A\otimes 1)(j_{0},\ldots,j_{n})
. For all of these three actions,
\varphi
behaves on morphisms as one would expect. The definitions of these actions make it clear that morally
\varphi
is acting by the associated isomorphism
A
on all the relevant
d
–tuples and thus it is obvious that all of these actions commute with the respective structure morphisms down to
\mathbb{P}^{n}
, which is what we mean by saying the actions are compatible with the action on
\mathbb{P}^{n}
. It is also clear that the maps

\mathcal{O}|_{\mathbb{P}^{n}}\to\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{n}}(1)^{d}\to\mathcal{T}_{\mathbb{P}^{n}/S}

in the Euler exact sequence are equivariant with respect to these
\mathbf{PGL}_{d}
–actions. Since the category of stacks satisfies descent, for example as explained in
[
Bre
, Example 1.11(i)]
, these
\mathbf{PGL}_{d}
–actions can be used to simultaneously twist
\mathbb{P}^{n}
as well as the exact sequence of sheaves on
\mathbb{P}^{n}
with respect to
\mathbf{PGL}_{d}
–torsors.

Lemma 5.1
.

Let
P\to S
be a Severi-Brauer scheme of constant relative dimension
n
with corresponding
\mathbf{PGL}_{d}
–torsor
\mathcal{K}
. Then, the twisting the diagram of stack morphisms

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{O}|_{\mathbb{P}^{n}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{n}}(1)^{d}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{T}_{\mathbb{P}^{n}/S}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{P}^{n}}

produces a diagram of stack morphisms over
\Sch_{P}
represented by the exact sequence of
\mathcal{O}|_{P}
–modules

0\to\mathcal{O}|_{P}\to\mathcal{F}(P)\to\mathcal{T}_{P/S}\to 0.

Proof.

Since the
\mathbf{PGL}_{d}
action on both the structure sheaf and the tangent space of
\mathbb{P}^{n}
were dictated by the action on
\mathbb{P}^{n}
, we will obtain

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}\wedge^{\mathbf{PGL}_{d}}\mathcal{O}|_{\mathbb{P}^{n}}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}|_{\Sch_{P}},\text{ and}

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}\wedge^{\mathbf{PGL}_{d}}\mathcal{T}_{\mathbb{P}^{n}/S}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{T}_{\Sch_{P}/S}

after twisting. Furthermore, since the Euler exact sequence is
\mathbf{PGL}_{d}
–equivariant we will have morphisms

\mathcal{O}|_{\Sch_{P}}\to\mathcal{K}\wedge^{\mathbf{PGL}_{d}}\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{n}}(1)^{d}\to\mathcal{T}_{\Sch_{P}/S}

where all stacks are represented by vector bundles over
P
since they are vector bundles locally. Therefore, we obtain a sequence of
\mathcal{O}|_{P}
–modules on
\Sch_{P}
,

0\to\mathcal{O}|_{P}\to\mathcal{K}\wedge^{\mathbf{PGL}_{d}}\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}_{S}^{n}}(1)^{d}\to\mathcal{T}_{P/S}\to 0

which is exact and which is non-split since it is exact and non-split locally. However, by
[
Kol
, Theorem 81]
there is a unique such exact sequence, meaning that

\mathcal{K}\wedge^{\mathbf{PGL}_{d}}\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}_{S}^{n}}(1)^{d}\cong\mathcal{F}(P).

This finishes the proof.
∎

5.B.iv.
Induced Action on the Pushforward

Throughout the remained of this section we fix
f\colon\mathbb{P}_{S}^{n}\to S
as the structure morphism of relative projective space. Given a sheaf
\mathcal{F}\colon\Sch_{\mathbb{P}_{S}^{n}}\to\Sets
, its pushforward with respect to
f
is the sheaf

\displaystyle f_{*}(\mathcal{F})\colon\Sch_{S}

\displaystyle\to\Sets

\displaystyle U

\displaystyle\mapsto\mathcal{F}(U\times_{S}\mathbb{P}_{S}^{n}).

The choices of fiber products
U\times_{S}\mathbb{P}_{S}^{n}
for each
U\in\Sch_{S}
can be encoded as a functor
\Sch_{S}\to\Sch_{\mathbb{P}_{S}^{n}}\cong\mathbb{P}^{n}
. In particular, we use the functor

\displaystyle p\colon\Sch_{S}

\displaystyle\to\mathbb{P}^{n}

\displaystyle U

\displaystyle\mapsto(\mathbb{P}_{U}^{n},\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}_{U}^{n}}(1),(x_{0},\ldots,x_{n}))

and which acts naturally on morphisms. Then, if
\mathfrak{F}\to\mathbb{P}^{n}
is the stack associated to the sheaf
\mathcal{F}
on
\mathbb{P}_{S}^{n}
, then the pushforward
f_{*}(\mathcal{F})
on
S
corresponds to the fiber product

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p^{-1}(\mathfrak{F})=\Sch_{S}\times_{\mathbb{P}^{n}}\mathfrak{F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{F}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sch_{S}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{P}^{n}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}

Now, given
\varphi\in\mathbf{PGL}_{d}(S)
and the corresponding isomorphism
\varphi\colon\mathbb{P}^{n}\to\mathbb{P}^{n}
, the functor
\varphi\circ p\colon\Sch_{S}\to\mathbb{P}^{n}
encodes a different choice of fiber product with
\mathbb{P}_{S}^{n}
for each
U\in\Sch_{S}
. In particular, if
\varphi
corresponds to the isomorphism
A\colon\mathcal{O}^{d}\to\mathcal{I}^{d}
for a line bundle
\mathcal{I}
on
S
, then

(\varphi\circ p)(U)=(\mathbb{P}_{U}^{n},\mathcal{I}|_{U}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}|_{\mathbb{P}_{U}^{n}}}\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}_{U}^{n}}(1),((Ax)_{1},\ldots,(Ax)_{n}))

where
x=(x_{0},\ldots,x_{n})\in\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}_{U}^{n}}(1)^{d}
and
(Ax)_{i}
is the
i^{\textrm{th}}
component of
(A\otimes 1)(x)
. Going back to schemes for a moment, if the “obvious” choice of fiber product
U\times_{S}\mathbb{P}_{S}^{n}
given by
p
is the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{P}_{U}^{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{P}_{S}^{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}

i.e.,
(\mathbb{P}_{U}^{n}\xrightarrow{\alpha}\mathbb{P}_{S}^{n})=p(U)=(\mathbb{P}_{U}^{n},\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}_{U}^{n}}(1),(x_{0},\ldots,x_{n}))
, then the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{P}_{U}^{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{P}_{S}^{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{P}_{S}^{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}

corresponds to the choice
(\varphi\circ p)(U)
. Since these are both fiber products for the same diagram, there is a unique isomorphism of schemes
\varphi|_{U}\colon\mathbb{P}_{U}^{n}\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}\mathbb{P}_{U}^{n}
between the two fiber products. In particular, for each
U\in\Sch_{S}
there is an isomorphism in
\mathbb{P}^{n}

(\varphi|_{U},\Id)\colon(\mathbb{P}_{U}^{n},\mathcal{I}|_{U}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}|_{\mathbb{P}_{U}^{n}}}\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}_{U}^{n}}(1),((Ax)_{1},\ldots,(Ax)_{n}))\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}(\mathbb{P}_{U}^{n},\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}_{U}^{n}}(1),(x_{0},\ldots,x_{n}))

where the isomorphism between the line bundles is simply the identity because

\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}_{U}^{n}}(1)|_{\varphi|_{U}}=\mathcal{I}|_{U}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}|_{\mathbb{P}_{U}^{n}}}\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}_{U}^{n}}(1)

and the restriction map
\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}_{U}^{n}}(1)(\varphi|_{U})
sends
x_{i}\mapsto(Ax)_{i}
. These morphisms give us a natural isomorphism of functors
\varphi\circ p\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}p
, and therefore also a canonical isomorphism of
\Sch_{S}
–stacks

\can\colon(\varphi\circ p)^{-1}(\mathfrak{F})\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}p^{-1}(\mathfrak{F})

where the isomorphism between the fibers
(\varphi\circ p)^{-1}(\mathfrak{F})(U)\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}p^{-1}(\mathfrak{F})(U)
is given by the restriction maps

\mathcal{F}(\varphi|_{U}^{-1})\colon\mathcal{F}((\varphi\circ p)(U))\to\mathcal{F}(p(U))

of the sheaf
\mathcal{F}
along the inverse of the isomorphism. In other words, this is the equivalent of canonical isomorphism
f_{*}(\varphi^{*}(\mathcal{F}))\cong f_{*}(\mathcal{F})
.

The above is applicable to any stacks
\mathfrak{F}\to\mathbb{P}^{n}
, but we now apply it to the case of
\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{n}}(1)^{d}\to\mathbb{P}^{n}
. It is a standard fact that
f_{*}(\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}_{S}^{n}}(1))\cong\mathcal{O}^{d}
where the sections
x_{0},\ldots,x_{n}
form the
\mathcal{O}
–module basis. In our setup we see the same thing since the objects in the fiber
p^{-1}(\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{n}}(1))(U)
are of the form

(\mathbb{P}_{U}^{n},\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}_{U}^{n}}(1),(x_{0},\ldots,x_{n}),v\in\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}_{U}^{n}}(1)(\mathbb{P}_{U}^{n})).

Removing unnecessary information, we can consider
p^{-1}(\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{n}}(1))
as having objects of the form

(U,v\in\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}_{U}^{n}}(1)(\mathbb{P}_{U}^{n})=\Span_{\mathcal{O}(U)}(\{x_{0},\ldots,x_{n}\})

which gives an obvious isomorphism
p^{-1}(\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{n}}(1))\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}\mathcal{O}^{d}
. We may also identify
(\varphi\circ p)^{-1}(\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{n}}(1))
this way.

Lemma 5.2
.

Given
\varphi\in\mathbf{PGL}_{d}(S)
as above, there is a natural isomorphism
(\varphi\circ p)^{-1}(\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{n}}(1))\cong\mathcal{I}^{d}
over
\Sch_{S}
. Using both this isomorphism and the identification
p^{-1}(\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{n}}(1))\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}\mathcal{O}^{d}
, the canonical isomorphism

\can\colon(\varphi\circ p)^{-1}(\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{n}}(1))\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}p^{-1}(\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{n}}(1))

appears as

A^{-1}\colon\mathcal{I}^{d}\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}\mathcal{O}^{d}.

Proof.

The objects in the fiber
(\varphi\circ p)^{-1}(\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{n}}(1))(U)
are of the form

(\mathbb{P}_{U}^{n},\mathcal{I}|_{U}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}|_{\mathbb{P}_{U}^{n}}}\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}_{U}^{n}}(1),((Ax)_{1},\ldots,(Ax)_{n}),v\in\big(\mathcal{I}|_{U}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}|_{\mathbb{P}_{U}^{n}}}\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}_{U}^{n}}(1)\big)(\mathbb{P}_{U}^{n}))

where the only variation is in the choice of
v
. Therefore,
(\varphi\circ p)^{-1}(\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{n}}(1))(U)
is equivalent to the stack with objects

(U,v\in\big(\mathcal{I}|_{U}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}|_{\mathbb{P}_{U}^{n}}}\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}_{U}^{n}}(1)\big)(\mathbb{P}_{U}^{n}))

where here

\big(\mathcal{I}|_{U}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}|_{\mathbb{P}_{U}^{n}}}\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}_{U}^{n}}(1)\big)(\mathbb{P}_{U}^{n})=\bigoplus_{i=0}^{n}\mathcal{I}\cdot x_{i}

giving an isomorphism
(\varphi\circ p)^{-1}(\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{n}}(1))\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}\mathcal{I}^{d}
.

The canonical isomorphism

\can\colon(\varphi\circ p)^{-1}(\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{n}}(1))\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}p^{-1}(\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{n}}(1))

comes from restriction along
\varphi^{-1}
within
\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{n}}(1)
. By definition, the restrictions in
\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{n}}(1)
along
\varphi
correspond to maps of the form
A\otimes 1
, and the canonical isomorphism corresponds to
A^{-1}\otimes 1
which after the pushforward is simply

A^{-1}\colon\mathcal{I}^{d}\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}\mathcal{O}^{d}

as claimed.
∎

If we consider
\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{n}}(1)^{d}
as consisting of column vectors and
p^{-1}(\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{n}}(1))\cong\mathcal{O}^{d}
as consisting of rows, and likewise for
(\varphi\circ p)^{-1}(\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{n}}(1))
then we obtain
\mathcal{O}
–module isomorphisms

(30)

\displaystyle p^{-1}(\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{n}}(1)^{d})

\displaystyle\cong\Mat_{d}(\mathcal{O}),\text{ and}

\displaystyle(\varphi\circ p)^{-1}(\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{n}}(1)^{d})

\displaystyle\cong\Mat_{d}(\mathcal{I}).

Since the canonical isomorphism
(\varphi\circ p)^{-1}(\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{n}}(1)^{d})\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}p^{-1}(\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{n}}(1)^{d})
only acts within each row, writing
A^{-1}\in\Mat_{d}(\mathcal{I}^{*})
as a matrix with entries in the dual line bundle, this canonical isomorphism simply appears as right multiplication by
A^{-1}
;

\displaystyle\can\colon\Mat_{d}(\mathcal{I})

\displaystyle\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}\Mat_{d}(\mathcal{O})

\displaystyle T

\displaystyle\mapsto TA^{-1}.

As a final step, because the action of
\mathbf{PGL}_{d}
on
\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{n}}(1)^{d}
is compatible with the action on
\mathbb{P}^{n}
, we have the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{n}}(1)^{d}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{n}}(1)^{d}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{P}^{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{P}^{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}

which can be extended to the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{n}}(1)^{d}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi^{-1}(\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{n}}(1)^{d})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{n}}(1)^{d}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{P}^{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{P}^{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sch_{S}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}

where
\varphi^{\prime}
is the map uniquely determined by
\varphi^{-1}(\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{n}}(1)^{d})=\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{n}}(1)^{d}\times_{\varphi}\mathbb{P}^{n}
being a fiber product and the maps
p\colon\Sch_{S}\to\mathbb{P}^{n}
are dashed since the diagram is only commutative without them. Hence, for each
\varphi\in\mathbf{PGL}_{d}(S)
we get an automorphism

p^{-1}(\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{n}}(1)^{d})\xrightarrow{p^{-1}(\varphi^{\prime})}(\varphi\circ p)^{-1}(\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{n}}(1)^{d})\xrightarrow{\can}p^{-1}(\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{n}}(1)^{d})

which defines an induced
\mathbf{PGL}_{d}
action on
p^{-1}(\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{n}}(1)^{d})
compatible with the action on
\mathbb{P}^{n}
.

Lemma 5.3
.

Using the identification
p^{-1}(\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{n}}(1)^{d})\cong\Mat_{d}(\mathcal{O})
, the induced action of
\mathbf{PGL}_{d}
on
p^{-1}(\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{n}}(1)^{d})
defined above agrees with the usual
\mathbf{PGL}_{d}
–action on
\Mat_{d}(\mathcal{O})
by
\mathcal{O}
–algebra isomorphisms.

Proof.

In the definition of the
\mathbf{PGL}_{d}
action on
\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{n}}(1)^{d}
given in (
29
),
\varphi
acts on the chosen section
v\in(\mathcal{I}|_{\mathbb{P}_{U}^{n}}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}|_{\mathbb{P}_{U}^{n}}}\mathcal{O}|_{\mathbb{P}_{U}^{n}}(1))^{d}(U)
by
A\otimes 1
. If we consider
v
as a column vector, then
A\otimes 1
may be written as a
d\times d
matrix with entries in
(\mathcal{I}|_{\mathbb{P}_{S}^{n}}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}|_{\mathbb{P}_{S}^{n}}}\mathcal{O}|_{\mathbb{P}_{S}^{n}}(1))(\mathbb{P}_{S}^{n})=\mathcal{O}(S)
and it acts on
v
simply by left multiplication. Then, when we apply the isomorphisms of (
30
) the map
p^{-1}(\varphi^{\prime})
acts on the column of rows as left multiplication by
A
, i.e. it acts simply as

\displaystyle\Mat_{d}(\mathcal{O})

\displaystyle\to\Mat_{d}(\mathcal{I})

\displaystyle T

\displaystyle\mapsto AT.

As discussed above, the canonical isomorphism
(\varphi\circ p)^{-1}(\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{n}}(1)^{d})\to p^{-1}(\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{n}}(1)^{d})
appears as right multiplication by
A^{-1}
and so together the induced action of
\varphi
on
\Mat_{d}(\mathcal{O})
is given by

\displaystyle\varphi\colon\Mat_{d}(\mathcal{O})

\displaystyle\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}\Mat_{d}(\mathcal{O})

\displaystyle T

\displaystyle\mapsto ATA^{-1}.

This is of course equal to the usual action of
\varphi
on
\Mat_{d}(\mathcal{O})
, so we are done.
∎

This discussion culminates in the promised punchline.

Proposition 5.4
.

Let
f\colon P\to S
be a Severi-Brauer scheme of constant relative dimension
n
. Let
\mathcal{F}(P)
be the unique vector bundle on
P
fitting into the Euler exact sequence. Then, the pushforward
f_{*}(F(P))
has a natural structure as an Azumaya
\mathcal{O}
–algebra. Furthermore, for an Azumaya
\mathcal{O}
–algebra
\mathcal{A}
of constant degree
d=n+1
,

f_{*}\big(\mathcal{F}(\SB(\mathcal{A}))\big)\cong\mathcal{A}.

Proof.

As detailed above, in the split case we have an
\mathcal{O}
–module isomorphism
f_{*}(\mathcal{F}(\mathbb{P}_{S}^{n}))\cong\Mat_{d}(\mathcal{O})
. We use this isomorphism to equip
f_{*}(\mathcal{F}(\mathbb{P}_{S}^{n}))
with the structure of an Azumaya
\mathcal{O}
–algebra.

Now, let
\mathcal{K}
be the
\mathbf{PGL}_{d}
–torsor corresponding to
P\to S
. Using
5.1
, we may simultaneously twist
\mathbb{P}_{S}^{n}
and
\mathcal{F}(\mathbb{P}_{S}^{n})
by
\mathcal{K}
to obtain
P
and
\mathcal{F}(P)
. Because the induced
\mathbf{PGL}_{d}
–action on
f_{*}(\mathcal{F}(\mathbb{P}_{S}^{n}))
is compatible with the action on
\mathbb{P}_{S}^{n}
, we obtain that

f_{*}(\mathcal{F}(P))=f_{*}\big(\mathcal{K}\wedge^{\mathbf{PGL}_{d}}\mathcal{F}(\mathbb{P}_{S}^{n})\big)=\mathcal{K}\wedge^{\mathbf{PGL}_{d}}f_{*}(\mathcal{F}(\mathbb{P}_{S}^{n})).

The action of
\mathbf{PGL}_{d}
on
f_{*}(\mathcal{F}(\mathbb{P}_{S}^{n}))
is by Azumaya
\mathcal{O}
–algebra automorphisms, so
f_{*}(\mathcal{F}(P))
naturally inherits the structure of an Azumaya
\mathcal{O}
–algebra.

In the case
P=\SB(\mathcal{A})
, then the torsor
\mathcal{K}
is also the torsor corresponding to the Azumaya
\mathcal{O}
–algebra
\mathcal{A}
. Hence,

f_{*}(\mathcal{F}(P))=\mathcal{K}\wedge^{\mathbf{PGL}_{d}}f_{*}(\mathcal{F}(\mathbb{P}_{S}^{n}))=\mathcal{K}\wedge^{\mathbf{PGL}_{d}}\Mat_{d}(\mathcal{O})\cong\mathcal{A}.

This finishes the proof.
∎

This provides a variation on Quillen’s construction, since instead of the usual categorical equivalence of (
28
), we may also use the equivalence

\displaystyle\mathfrak{Azu}_{d}\leftrightarrow

\displaystyle\mathfrak{SB}_{n}

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}\mapsto

\displaystyle\SB(\mathcal{A})

\displaystyle f_{*}\big(\mathcal{F}(P)\big)\reflectbox{$\mapsto$}

\displaystyle P.

with the algebra structure on
f_{*}\big(\mathcal{F}(P)\big)
given as in
5.4
.

5.C.
Outer Type Severi-Brauer Schemes

Unsurprisingly, the relationship between inner type Severi-Brauer schemes and our upcoming definition of outer type Severi-Brauer schemes closely resembles the relationship between Azumaya algebras and Azumaya algebras with an involution of the second kind.

Recall that an Azumaya algebra with involution of the second kind is the data of
(f\colon L\to S,\mathcal{B},\tau)
where
L\to S
is a degree
2
étale cover with a canonical order two
S
–automorphism
i\colon L\to L
,
\mathcal{B}
is an Azumaya algebra
\mathcal{O}|_{L}
–algebra, and
\tau\colon f_{*}(\mathcal{B})\to f_{*}(\mathcal{B})
is an
i^{*}
–semilinear involution of the
f_{*}(\mathcal{O}|_{L})=\mathcal{L}
algebra
f_{*}(\mathcal{B})
. The data of such a semilinear involution is equivalent to an
\mathcal{L}
–linear anti-isomorphism
f_{*}(\mathcal{B})\to f_{*}(\mathcal{B})\otimes_{i^{*}}\mathcal{L}
, which in turn is equivalent to an
\mathcal{L}
–linear isomorphism

\tilde{\tau}\colon f_{*}(\mathcal{B})\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}f_{*}(\mathcal{B})^{\mathrm{op}}\otimes_{i^{*}}\mathcal{L}.

We consider the category of Azumaya
\mathcal{O}
–algebras of degree
d
as a subcategory of the category of Azumaya algebras of degree
d
with involution of the second kind by sending an Azumaya
\mathcal{O}
–algebra
\mathcal{A}
to

(S\sqcup S\to S,(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{A}^{\mathrm{op}}),\tau)

where the canonical automorphism of
S\sqcup S
is the switch and
\tau\colon\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{A}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{A}^{\mathrm{op}}
is the switch-opposite, i.e.,
\tau(a,b^{\mathrm{op}})=(b,a^{\mathrm{op}})
. Here, for the equivalent
\mathcal{O}\times\mathcal{O}
–linear map we simply have that
\tilde{\tau}=\Id
. Thus, mirroring this setup for the Severi-Brauer scheme
\SB(\mathcal{A})
, the associated outer-type Severi-Brauer scheme associated to
\mathcal{A}
should be the data of

\SB(\mathcal{A})\sqcup\SB(\mathcal{A}^{\mathrm{op}})\to S\sqcup S

along with an
S\sqcup S
–scheme isomorphism, in this case the identity,

\SB(\mathcal{A})\sqcup\SB(\mathcal{A}^{\mathrm{op}})\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}\big(\SB(\mathcal{A})^{\mathrm{op}}\sqcup\SB(\mathcal{A}^{\mathrm{op}})^{\mathrm{op}})\times_{\textrm{sw}}(S\sqcup S).

Equivalently, since we are now working with schemes we can write this as the data of an
S
–scheme isomorphism
\tau
such that the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\SB(\mathcal{A})\sqcup\SB(\mathcal{A}^{\mathrm{op}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\big(\SB(\mathcal{A})\sqcup\SB(\mathcal{A}^{\mathrm{op}})\big)^{\mathrm{op}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S\sqcup S}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S\sqcup S}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau}
sw

is a fiber product diagram. In the case when
\mathcal{A}=\Mat_{d}(\mathcal{O})
, we take the split object to be

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{P}_{S}^{n}\sqcup\mathbb{P}_{S}^{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{P}_{S}^{n}\sqcup\mathbb{P}_{S}^{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S\sqcup S}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S\sqcup S}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau_{0}}
sw

where
\tau_{0}
is simply the switch since we may take
(\mathbb{P}_{S}^{n})^{\mathrm{op}}=\mathbb{P}_{S}^{n}
due to the isomorphism
\Mat_{d}(\mathcal{O})\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}\Mat_{d}(\mathcal{O})^{\mathrm{op}}
given by the transpose.

Thus, generalizing this setup to match the situation with
(L\to S,\mathcal{B},\tau)
above, we arrive the following natural definition.

Definition 5.5
.

An
outer type Severi-Brauer scheme
of constant relative dimension
n
is the data of
(f\colon L\to S,P\to L,\tau)
where

(i)

L\to S
is a degree
2
étale cover with canonical automorphism
i\colon L\to L
,

(ii)

P\to L
is a Severi-Brauer scheme over
L
of constant relative dimension
n
,

(iii)

\tau\colon P\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}P^{\mathrm{op}}
is an
S
–scheme isomorphism making the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P^{\mathrm{op}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}

commute,

and all such that étale locally over
S
this data is isomorphic to
(S\sqcup S\to S,\mathbb{P}_{S}^{n}\sqcup\mathbb{P}_{S}^{n},\tau_{0})
.

From an Azumaya algebra with involution of the second kind
(f\colon L\to S,\mathcal{B},\tau)
, one may consider
\mathcal{B}
simply as an Azumaya
\mathcal{O}|_{L}
–algebra and obtain the Severi-Brauer scheme
\SB(\mathcal{B})\to L
. However, the involution
\tau\colon f_{*}(\mathcal{B})\to f_{*}(\mathcal{B})
is only defined on the pushforward and does not immediately lead to a suitable map
\SB(\mathcal{B})\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}\SB(\mathcal{B}^{\mathrm{op}})
needed to define an outer-type Severi-Brauer scheme. Instead, we construct the associated outer type Severi-Brauer scheme as in the inner case, from fibers of the type morphism.

Lemma 5.6
.

Let
(f\colon L\to S,\mathcal{B},\tau)
be an Azumaya algebra with involution of the second type of constant degree
d
and set
\mathbf{G}=\mathbf{U}_{(\mathcal{B},\tau)}
. There is an isomorphism of sheaves
\mathcal{G}\mathcal{S}\mathcal{B}^{-}_{(\mathcal{B},\tau)}\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}\mathcal{G}\mathcal{S}\mathcal{B}^{-}_{(\mathcal{B}^{\mathrm{op}},\tau)}
which fits into the commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{G}\mathcal{S}\mathcal{B}^{-}_{(\mathcal{B},\tau)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{G}\mathcal{S}\mathcal{B}^{-}_{(\mathcal{B}^{\mathrm{op}},\tau)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{O}\hskip-1.29167pt\textit{f}(\mathcal{D}\hskip-1.72218pt\textit{y\hskip-1.29167ptn}(\mathbf{G}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{O}\hskip-1.29167pt\textit{f}(\mathcal{D}\hskip-1.72218pt\textit{y\hskip-1.29167ptn}(\mathbf{G}))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\hskip 6.02777pt}^{\vee}}

where the downward maps are the respective type morphisms as given in
3.33
.

Proof.

We show that we have an isomorphism fitting into such a diagram for the underlying presheaves of constant rank flags. Set
\mathcal{L}=f_{*}(\mathcal{O}|_{L})
. Recall from
3.20
that
\mathcal{G}\mathcal{S}\mathcal{B}^{-}_{(\mathcal{B},\tau)}=(\mathcal{C}\hskip-1.72218pt\mathcal{I}\hskip-1.72218pt\textit{d\hskip-1.07639pte\hskip-1.07639pta\hskip-1.07639ptl}_{(\mathcal{B},\tau)})^{\sharp}
where
\mathcal{C}\hskip-1.72218pt\mathcal{I}\hskip-1.72218pt\textit{d\hskip-1.07639pte\hskip-1.07639pta\hskip-1.07639ptl}_{(\mathcal{B},\tau)}
consists of flags of right
\mathcal{L}
–ideals of
f_{*}(\mathcal{B})
with strictly positive
\mathcal{L}
–gap between subsequent components of the flag and such that

(0\subseteq\mathcal{I}_{1}\subseteq\ldots\subseteq\mathcal{I}_{\ell}\subseteq f_{*}(\mathcal{B}))=(0\subseteq\tau({{}^{0}}\mathcal{I}_{\ell})\subseteq\ldots\subseteq\tau({{}^{0}}\mathcal{I}_{1})\subseteq f_{*}(\mathcal{B})).

For an
\mathcal{L}
–right ideal
\mathcal{I}\subseteq f_{*}(\mathcal{B})
, the left annihilator
{{}^{0}}\mathcal{I}
is an
\mathcal{L}
–left ideal which may therefore be viewed as an
\mathcal{L}
–right ideal in
f_{*}(\mathcal{B}^{\mathrm{op}})
. Furthermore, left annihilator of
{{}^{0}}\mathcal{I}
in
f_{*}(\mathcal{B}^{\mathrm{op}})
is the same as the right annihilator of
{{}^{0}}\mathcal{I}
in
f_{*}(\mathcal{B})
, which is just
\mathcal{I}
viewed as an
\mathcal{L}
–left ideal in
f_{*}(\mathcal{B}^{\mathrm{op}})
. Therefore, if we consider

0\subseteq{{}^{0}}\mathcal{I}_{\ell}\subseteq\ldots\subseteq{{}^{0}}\mathcal{I}_{1}\subseteq f_{*}(\mathcal{B}^{\mathrm{op}})

as a flag of right ideals, then
\tau\big({{}^{0}}({{}^{0}}\mathcal{I}_{j})\big)=\tau(\mathcal{I}_{j})={{}^{0}}\mathcal{I}_{\ell+1-j}
due to the symmetric property of the original flag. The required property on
\mathcal{L}
–gaps will also be preserved, hence

(0\subseteq{{}^{0}}\mathcal{I}_{\ell}\subseteq\ldots\subseteq{{}^{0}}\mathcal{I}_{1}\subseteq f_{*}(\mathcal{B}^{\mathrm{op}}))\in\mathcal{C}\hskip-1.72218pt\mathcal{I}\hskip-1.72218pt\textit{d\hskip-1.07639pte\hskip-1.07639pta\hskip-1.07639ptl}_{(\mathcal{B}^{\mathrm{op}},\tau)}

and this clearly defines an isomorphism of presheaves
\mathcal{C}\hskip-1.72218pt\mathcal{I}\hskip-1.72218pt\textit{d\hskip-1.07639pte\hskip-1.07639pta\hskip-1.07639ptl}_{(\mathcal{B},\tau)}\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}\mathcal{C}\hskip-1.72218pt\mathcal{I}\hskip-1.72218pt\textit{d\hskip-1.07639pte\hskip-1.07639pta\hskip-1.07639ptl}_{(\mathcal{B}^{\mathrm{op}},\tau)}
.

Sufficiently locally, when
\mathcal{C}\hskip-1.72218pt\mathcal{I}\hskip-1.72218pt\textit{d\hskip-1.07639pte\hskip-1.07639pta\hskip-1.07639ptl}_{(\mathcal{B},\tau)}
is isomorphic to
\mathcal{C}\hskip-1.72218pt\mathcal{I}\hskip-1.72218pt\textit{d\hskip-1.07639pte\hskip-1.07639pta\hskip-1.07639ptl}_{((\Mat_{d}(\mathcal{O}),\Mat_{d}(\mathcal{O})),\tau_{d})}
this map appears as

\displaystyle(0\subseteq\mathcal{I}_{1}\times({{}^{0}}\mathcal{I}_{\ell})^{t}\subseteq\ldots\subseteq\mathcal{I}_{\ell}\times({{}^{0}}\mathcal{I}_{1})^{t}\subseteq\Mat_{d}(\mathcal{O})\times\Mat_{d}(\mathcal{O}))

\displaystyle\mapsto

\displaystyle(0\subseteq({{}^{0}}\mathcal{I}_{\ell})\times\mathcal{I}_{1}^{t}\subseteq\ldots\subseteq({{}^{0}}\mathcal{I}_{1})\times\mathcal{I}_{\ell}^{t}\subseteq\Mat_{d}(\mathcal{O})^{\mathrm{op}}\times\Mat_{d}(\mathcal{O}))^{\mathrm{op}}).

Since for a right ideal
\mathcal{I}\subseteq\Mat_{d}(\mathcal{O})
of constant rank,
\rank_{\mathcal{O}}({{}^{0}}\mathcal{I})=d^{2}-\rank(\mathcal{I})
, we see that we have a commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}\hskip-1.72218pt\mathcal{I}\hskip-1.72218pt\textit{d\hskip-1.07639pte\hskip-1.07639pta\hskip-1.07639ptl}_{((\Mat_{d}(\mathcal{O}),\Mat_{d}(\mathcal{O})),\tau_{d})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{C}\hskip-1.72218pt\mathcal{I}\hskip-1.72218pt\textit{d\hskip-1.07639pte\hskip-1.07639pta\hskip-1.07639ptl}_{((\Mat_{d}(\mathcal{O}),\Mat_{d}(\mathcal{O}))^{\mathrm{op}},\tau_{d})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{P}_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\hskip 6.02777pt}^{\vee}}

where the downward maps are those defining the type morphism. Hence, after sheafifying and twisting we obtain an isomorphism
\mathcal{G}\mathcal{S}\mathcal{B}^{-}_{(\mathcal{B},\tau)}\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}\mathcal{G}\mathcal{S}\mathcal{B}^{-}_{(\mathcal{B}^{\mathrm{op}},\tau)}
which fits into the commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{G}\mathcal{S}\mathcal{B}^{-}_{(\mathcal{B},\tau)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{G}\mathcal{S}\mathcal{B}^{-}_{(\mathcal{B}^{\mathrm{op}},\tau)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{O}\hskip-1.29167pt\textit{f}(\mathcal{D}\hskip-1.72218pt\textit{y\hskip-1.29167ptn}(\mathbf{G}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{O}\hskip-1.29167pt\textit{f}(\mathcal{D}\hskip-1.72218pt\textit{y\hskip-1.29167ptn}(\mathbf{G}))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\hskip 6.02777pt}^{\vee}}

as claimed, finishing the proof.
∎

Because the sheaf
\mathcal{G}\mathcal{S}\mathcal{B}^{-}_{(\mathcal{B},\tau)}
is isomorphic to the sheaf of parabolic subgroups
\mathcal{P}\hskip-1.93747pt\textit{a\hskip-0.6458ptr}_{\mathbf{G}}
for
\mathbf{G}=\mathbf{U}_{(\mathcal{B},\tau)}
, we know it is representable by some scheme
X\in\Sch_{S}
. Likewise,
\mathcal{G}\mathcal{S}\mathcal{B}^{-}_{(\mathcal{B}^{\mathrm{op}},\tau)}
is represented by some
X^{\prime}
. Thus, translating the diagram of
5.6
, we have a diagram

(31)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Of(\Dyn(\mathbf{G}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Of(\Dyn(\mathbf{G}))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Of(\Dyn(i))}

where
\Of(\Dyn(i))
is as in (
9
). In particular, it acts as the order two isomorphism
i\colon L\to L
on the
L_{((1),(n))}
component of
\Of(\Dyn(\mathbf{G}))
. Note that this is the component which is locally isomorphic to
S_{(1)}\sqcup S_{(n)}
. It is not the
S_{(1,n)}
component which is isomorphic to
S
since
(1,n)^{\vee}=(1,n)
.

Lemma 5.7
.

The fiber over the component
L_{((1),(n))}
of the map
X\to\Of(\Dyn(\mathbf{G}))
is isomorphic to
\SB(\mathcal{B})\to L
.

Proof.

We argue via twisting. The Azumaya algebra with involution of the second kind
(f\colon L\to S,\mathcal{B},\tau)
corresponds to a
\mathbf{PGL}_{d}\rtimes\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
cocycle over some cover of
S
, say over
\{T_{i}\to S\}_{i\in I}
and the cocycle
(\varphi_{ij})_{i,j\in I}
. Let
(\sigma_{ij})_{i,j\in I}
be the image of this cocycle in
\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
, which is a cocycle corresponding to the étale cover
L\to S
.

The fact that
(\varphi_{ij})_{i,j\in I}
is a cocycle for
(f\colon L\to S,\mathcal{B},\tau)
means that the local
\mathcal{O}|_{T_{ij}}
–algebra isomorphisms

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Mat_{d}(\mathcal{O}|_{T_{ij}})\times\Mat_{d}(\mathcal{O}|_{T_{ij}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Mat_{d}(\mathcal{O}|_{T_{ij}})\times\Mat_{d}(\mathcal{O}|_{T_{ij}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{O}|_{T_{ij}}\times\mathcal{O}|_{T_{ij}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{O}|_{T_{ij}}\times\mathcal{O}|_{T_{ij}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{ij}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma_{ij}}

glue
\mathcal{O}\times\mathcal{O}\to\Mat_{d}(\mathcal{O})\times\Mat_{d}(\mathcal{O})
into
\mathcal{L}\to f_{*}(\mathcal{B})
. We now convert this diagram to a diagram of schemes in two simultaneous steps. By
[
St
, Tag 01SA]
we may consider the
\sigma_{ij}
as isomorphisms of the scheme
T_{ij}\sqcup T_{ij}\to T_{ij}
. Then, under
[
St
, Tag 01SB]
, the algebra
\Mat_{d}(\mathcal{O}|_{T_{ij}})\times\Mat_{d}(\mathcal{O}|_{T_{ij}})
corresponds to the matrix algebra
\Mat_{d}(\mathcal{O}|_{T_{ij}\sqcup T_{ij}})
on the disjoint union. Then, since
\mathcal{L}\to f_{*}(\mathcal{B})
over
S
corresponds via
[
St
, Tag 01SB]
to the
\mathcal{O}|_{L}
–algebra
\mathcal{B}
over
L
, we see that simultaneously twisting
S\sqcup S
and
\Mat_{d}(\mathcal{O}|_{S\sqcup S})
by the cocycle produces
L
and
\mathcal{B}
.

In turn, we replace
\Mat_{d}(\mathcal{O}|_{T_{ij}\sqcup T_{ij}})
by its Sever-Brauer scheme, which is
\mathbb{P}_{T_{ij}\sqcup T_{ij}}^{n}\cong\mathbb{P}_{T_{ij}}^{n}\sqcup\mathbb{P}_{T_{ij}}^{n}
and obtain a diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{P}_{T_{ij}}^{n}\sqcup\mathbb{P}_{T_{ij}}^{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{P}_{T_{ij}}^{n}\sqcup\mathbb{P}_{T_{ij}}^{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{ij}\sqcup T_{ij}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T_{ij}\sqcup T_{ij}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{ij}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma_{ij}}

where
\mathbf{PGL}_{d}\rtimes\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
acts on
\mathbb{P}_{S}^{n}\sqcup\mathbb{P}_{S}^{n}
by
\psi\sqcup\theta^{\prime}(\psi)
for
\psi\in\mathbf{PGL}_{d}
and by the switch as usual for sections of
\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
. Recall that
\theta^{\prime}
is our chosen outer automorphism of
\mathbf{PGL}_{d}
corresponding to the inverse transpose on
\mathbf{GL}_{d}
. Due to the categorical equivalence between Azumaya algebra and Severi-Brauer schemes, our cocycle must twist
\mathbb{P}_{S}^{n}\sqcup\mathbb{P}_{S}^{n}\to S\sqcup S
into
\SB(\mathcal{B})\to L
.

Now, we consider the split case. By (
19
) we have the isomorphism

\mathcal{G}\mathcal{S}\mathcal{B}^{-}_{\Mat_{d}(\mathcal{O})}\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}\mathcal{G}\mathcal{S}\mathcal{B}^{-}_{((\Mat_{d}(\mathcal{O}),\Mat_{d}(\mathcal{O})),\tau_{d})}

which is used to define the type morphism in general. Since
\mathbf{U}_{((\Mat_{d}(\mathcal{O}),\Mat_{d}(\mathcal{O})),\tau_{d})}\cong\mathbf{GL}_{d}
, we know that
L=S\sqcup S
and

L_{((1),(n))}=S_{(1)}\sqcup S_{(n)}

in
\Of(\Dyn(\mathbf{GL}_{d}))
. We know for an Azumaya
\mathcal{O}
–algebra
\mathcal{A}
that both the fibers over
S_{(1)}
and
S_{(n)}
of
\mathcal{G}\mathcal{S}\mathcal{B}^{-}_{\mathcal{A}}
are represented by
\SB(\mathcal{A})
and
\SB(\mathcal{A}^{\mathrm{op}})
respectively. In particular, in the split case the fiber over
S_{(1)}\sqcup S_{(n)}
is

\mathbb{P}_{S}^{n}\sqcup\mathbb{P}_{S}^{n}\to S\sqcup S.

Here, the induced
\mathbf{PGL}_{d}
–action on
\mathbb{P}_{S}^{n}\sqcup\mathbb{P}_{S}^{n}
is by
\psi\sqcup\theta^{\prime}(\psi)
since the second
\mathbb{P}_{S}^{n}\cong\SB(\Mat_{d}(\mathcal{O})^{\mathrm{op}})
.

Finally,
3.23
tells us that
\mathcal{G}\mathcal{S}\mathcal{B}^{-}_{(\mathcal{B},\tau)}
is the twist of
\mathcal{G}\mathcal{S}\mathcal{B}^{-}_{((\Mat_{d}(\mathcal{O}),\Mat_{d}(\mathcal{O})),\tau_{d})}
by
(\varphi_{ij})_{i,j\in I}
, and therefore its fiber over
L_{((1),(n))}
will be the twist of the fiber over
S_{(1)}\sqcup S_{(n)}
in the split case. By the preceding discussion, this twist is
\SB(\mathcal{B})
, so we are done.
∎

Now when we consider the fiber over
L_{((1),(n)}
in (
31
), applying
5.7
twice tells us that we obtain a diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\SB(\mathcal{B})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\SB(\mathcal{B}^{\mathrm{op}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}

It is clear from construction that this is locally isomorphic to the split diagram as required in
5.5
.

Definition 5.8
.

Let
(f\colon L\to S,\mathcal{B},\tau)
be an Azumaya algebra with involution of the second type of constant degree
d
. We define the data of the diagram appearing above to be the
outer Severi-Brauer scheme
associated to
(f\colon L\to S,\mathcal{B},\tau)
, denoted
\SB(\mathcal{B},\tau)
.

There is also an outer version of Quillen’s construction which reconstructs an Azumaya algebra with involution of the second type from an outer Severi-Brauer scheme.

Theorem 5.9
.

Let
f\colon L\to T
be a degree
2
étale cover,
g\colon P\to L
a Severi-Brauer scheme and
\tau\colon P\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}P^{\mathrm{op}}
an isomorphism defining an outer type Severi-Brauer scheme. Then, there is a canonical isomorphism
\mathcal{F}(P)\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}\tau^{*}(\mathcal{F}(P^{\mathrm{op}}))
arising from
\tau
such that the pushfoward

f_{*}\big(g_{*}(\mathcal{F}(P))\big)\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}f_{*}\big(g_{*}(\tau^{*}(\mathcal{F}(P^{\mathrm{op}})))\big)\cong f_{*}\big(g_{*}(\mathcal{F}(P^{\mathrm{op}}))\big)\otimes_{i^{*}}\mathcal{L}

is equivalent to the information of an
i^{*}\colon\mathcal{L}\to\mathcal{L}
semi-linear involution

\tau^{\prime}\colon f_{*}\big(g_{*}(\mathcal{F}(P))\big)\to f_{*}\big(g_{*}(\mathcal{F}(P))\big)

making
(L\to S,g_{*}(\mathcal{F}(P)),\tau^{\prime})
an Azumaya algebra with involution of the second kind.

In the case that
P=\SB(\mathcal{B},\tau)
is the outer type Severi-Brauer scheme as defined in
5.8
, then
g_{*}(\mathcal{F}(P))=\mathcal{B}
and
\tau^{\prime}=\tau
.

Proof.

The action of
\mathbf{PGL}_{d}
defined on the pair
(\mathbb{P}_{S}^{n},\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}_{S}^{n}}(1))
used in
5.4
naturally extends to a
\mathbf{PGL}_{d}\rtimes\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
–action on the pair
(\mathbb{P}_{S}^{n}\sqcup\mathbb{P}_{S}^{n},\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}_{S}^{n}\sqcup\mathbb{P}_{S}^{n}}(1))
. By acting on the second component by
\theta^{\prime}(\varphi)
for
\varphi\in\mathbf{PGL}_{d}
. Then, if we consider the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}_{S}^{n}\sqcup\mathbb{P}_{S}^{n}}(1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}_{S}^{n}\sqcup\mathbb{P}_{S}^{n}}(1)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{P}_{S}^{n}\sqcup\mathbb{P}_{S}^{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{P}_{S}^{n}\sqcup\mathbb{P}_{S}^{n}}
sw
sw

it will be equivariant with repsect to the
\mathbf{PGL}_{d}\rtimes\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
-action provided we act by the opposite cocycle on the right. Hence, if we twist by a cocycle for
P\to L
, we obtain

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}(P)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{F}(P^{\mathrm{op}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P^{\mathrm{op}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau}

where
\tau^{\prime}
is equivalent to information of an isomorphism
\mathcal{F}(P)\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}\tau^{*}(\mathcal{F}(P^{\mathrm{op}}))
. Pushing this forward along
g
produces an
\mathcal{O}|_{L}
–algebra isomorphism

g_{*}(\mathcal{F}(P))\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}g_{*}(\tau^{*}(\mathcal{F}(P^{\mathrm{op}})))\cong i^{*}(g_{*}(\mathcal{F}(P^{\mathrm{op}}))).

using the Azumaya algebra structure on these pushforwards from
5.4
. Pushing forward further produces an
\mathcal{L}
–linear isomorphism

f_{*}\big(g_{*}(\mathcal{F}(P))\big)\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}f_{*}\big(g_{*}(\tau^{*}(\mathcal{F}(P^{\mathrm{op}})))\big)\cong f_{*}\big(g_{*}(\mathcal{F}(P^{\mathrm{op}}))\big)\otimes_{i^{*}}\mathcal{L}

as claimed, which is equivalent to an
\mathcal{L}
–semi-linear involution
\tau^{\prime}\colon f_{*}\big(g_{*}(\mathcal{F}(P))\big)\to f_{*}\big(g_{*}(\mathcal{F}(P))\big)
. Looking locally in the split case, since we applied both the switch and acted on the right by the opposite cocycle, the resulting map

\tau^{\prime}=f_{*}\big(g_{*}(\textrm{sw})\big)\colon\Mat_{d}(\mathcal{O})\times\Mat_{d}(\mathcal{O})\overset{\sim}{\longrightarrow}\Mat_{d}(\mathcal{O})\times\Mat_{d}(\mathcal{O})

will be the switch transpose
\tau_{d}
as in (
6
). Thus,
(L\to S,g_{*}(\mathcal{F}(P)),\tau^{\prime})
is an Azumaya algebra with involution of the second kind.

Since this process recovers
(S\sqcup S\to S,(\Mat_{d}(\mathcal{O}),\Mat_{d}(\mathcal{O}),\tau_{d})
in the split case and the induced
\mathbf{PGL}_{d}\rtimes\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
–action will be the usual one, it is clear that when we twist by a cocycle corresponding to
\SB(\mathcal{B},\tau)
we recover
(L\to S,\mathcal{B},\tau)
as claimed.
∎
